PREFACE 

Character Analysis is a subject that is of vital importance to everyone. In fact, every intelligent human being uses or tries to use character analysis in his daily life. 

We always obtain impressions of the people whom we meet; and sometimes our instinctive interpretations of their characteristics are correct. But often-too often-one is apt to form a hasty opinion which later proves to be wrong. 

From this comes the old adage that "appearances are deceitful," and many persons refuse to trust their own powers of observation. Too often one has the sad experience of believing a man to be honest; or judging him as a dangerous person, when he is exactly the opposite of what he is considered to be. 

This is proven by the fact that two persons will frequently form entirely different opinions of the same person. Both observe him, yet they judge oppositely. Hence we see that the power of observation is governed entirely by the mentality or mental impressions of the person who employs it. 

The reason is because the average observer is usually untrained. If, through experience, he has gained some natural ability in observation, still his methods are unsystematic.

The printed page tells us the author's thought; so do physical characteritics of individuals tell the student of physiognomy the sort of characters housed in the bodies especially designed for them by nature. Yet one must know the language in order to interpret correctly.

Suppose a story was written in a jargon of many languages: words of Latin, Greek, French, English, Russian, and Chinese thrown in at random. To the man who wrote it-who would of course be a scholar familiar with all those languages-the tale would be quite clear.

But the Frenchman would know only part of the story; the 

American who might try to read it would know another part; the 

Chinaman would pick up a few other ideas. When the notes of these people would be compared by the scholar who wrote the story, he would find that the impressions of the readers were quite dissimilar.

So it is with Character Analysis. Here is a wonderful study: 

a language of humanity, which everyone knows in part. But partial knowledge is dangerous; and while you are analyzing some

CHARACTER READING 

individual trait correctly, you may be overlooking a characteristic which is far more important. 

To make your observation really useful, you must learn to estimate the relative strength or weakness of the separate physical characteristics. This is of prime importance. In doing so, prejudices must be forgotten. All tall, dark, handsome men with black moustaches are not villains; nor are all attractive blonde women innocent and virtuous heroines.

The Character Analyst knows the salient physical features of human beings, with the mental and emotional traits for which they stand. Then he interprets the meaning of the combinations of physical signs just as one learns to read, first by mastering the alphabet and then by forming words from the letters. 

You can do the same; and you will, as you proceed, recognize certain factors which you knew existed. From the first lesson onward, your studies will be of value; and when you have completed the course, the sum of your knowledge will prove invaluable.

The purpose of the author is to teach instantaneous analysis, or Character Reading at a glance. While this work embraces every phase of Character Analysis, it has been designed for practical use, and extensive theories have been eliminated. 

Too many students of this subject delve into complexities which tend to obscure or hinder the study of the salient features. 

You cannot analyze the people you meet as if they were microscopic insects; you must judge them while you talk to them, and you must often decide upon their traits quickly-virtually, as we have said at a glance.

This can be done by anyone who makes a careful study of the methods given in the following pages. With Character Analysis at your disposal you will become an unerring student of human nature. As you sit in a trolley car, or a railway train, and look at the people about you, you will no longer wonder about them, or merely guess what kind of people they are: instead, you will find that their faces tell their stories. Each will prove as interesting as a book; you will have an index to their emotions, their likes and dislikes; their qualifications and their ambitions. In some cases you may even divine their occupations. 

So it will be with everyone whom you meet. You will have at your disposal the key to remarkable knowledge that will prove of immense benefit to yourself and to those whom you wish to befriend or aid.

No science is more helpful than Character Analysis; no study is more fascinating. 10

THE USES OF CHARACTER READING 

Apart from its interest as a study, and its aid in the development of one's natural intuition, Character Reading or Character Analysis has important uses which make it essential to the progressive person of modern times. 

These uses will become obvious as the student learns the fundamental principles of the science; but they are outlined here, and should be carefully read.

Every study should have a purpose. Many subjects taught in school are forgotten by the boys and girls who learn them, purely because they have no practical way of using them once they have acquired them.

This is true of everything, Character Reading included. To merely learn the methods of Character Analysis and then lay them aside would be a useless course. So the purpose of these lessons should be understood from the outset. This will not only be of value to the reader; it will prove an incentive to him in his study of the subject.

Domestic Relationship 

This is the first use of Character Reading. It applies to the family, and particularly to husband and wife. In the home, Character Reading is of immense value; it enables parents to watch with better understanding the development of their children, and guide them along paths that will make the most of their latent talents and individuality.

Conflicts sometimes occur with relatives who think they know each other well; whereas they know each other too well in certain ways, yet do not understand each other's real characteristics. 

Apply Character Analysis to members of your own family, and the results will surprise you. Studied purely as individuals, you will learn that they have abilities that you never suspected; and faults which you have noted in them will be explained—with the remedy.

Married life is often unfortunate because the husband and wife do not have a mutual understanding. Each expects the other to harmonize with him, or with her; and trouble often results purely because neither will admit the individuality of the other. 

No one can change the constitutional characteristics of an individual, but if you can read and understand that which is unchangeable, you will spare yourself the disappointments of unhappiness and disillusionment; and you will be more patient, 11

CHARACTER READING 

tolerant, and forgiving than you can possibly be without that knowledge and understanding.

Matrimony 

Character Reading in matrimonial life comes, of course, under the head of Domestic Relationship; but those who have been newly married or who contemplate matrimony can use Character 

Analysis to even better advantage.

The young husband and wife, not yet critical of one another, can learn much by studying the characteristics of each other. The knowledge that certain traits exist will give a forewarning of future developments, and will bring an understanding that will successfully forestall disagreements.

Engaged couples have a still greater opportunity to learn how well suited they are for happy marriage. Each should know the actual characteristics of the other; yet often certain traits do not become evident until after marriage. 

Matrimony, today, is an important undertaking. Prudent young people make provision beforehand: they save money; if possible, they buy a home. They should do more than this; they should plan the course they choose to follow, in order to lead a co-operative life. Character Reading affords the method by which this may be accomplished.

To young men and women who consider marriage, but who are not yet engaged, a keen Analysis of Character is the greatest aid in choosing a help-mate. Mutual attraction and common interest are accepted as guides, but they are by no means infallible. 

In fact they often lead people to overlook differences of opinions and ideals.

The young man who wants a wife; the young woman who desires a husband-both should apply Character Reading to its full extent. For by this scientific, accurate study, one can make a choice that is not guess-work; and the proverbial blindness of love can be avoided. Beauty or good looks will be judged not merely by facial characteristics, but by what those features signify. 

Social Life 

Friendships are important. Sometimes they become as enduring as life itself; other times they are forgotten and discarded. 

Why? Because they are often made through close association; through temporary conditions, which time may change. 12

Two people who are friends for a time may drift apart: other friends who have not seen each other for years will understand each other as well as ever.

This is not due to mutual interests; for many persons who have mutual interests dislike each other. It is due to harmony of characteristics.

Some people have the knack of making lasting friendships; this is due chiefly to their intuition. But others are unfortunate. 

They are often sorry that they were so friendly with certain people whom they now wish to forget. 

Good choice of friends is a wonderful ability; yet some people do not possess it, and they would give much to have that ability. They can gain it, easily and surely, through the employ ment of Character Reading. 

This is particularly important to persons who rush into friendships. Not so much the one who makes friends with everybody, but the type of person who has likes or dislikes and uses them at first sight. Such a person often makes an unfortunate guess; often regrets that he or she became friendly with one person and ignored another.

You may be of that type: in fact most people are, to some degree. Therefore, you can use Character Reading in the formation of friendships. Learn to read a person's character by the appearance of that person. Once you have acquired that ability, you will never again act foolishly. Someone who might have interested you before you had this wonderful knowledge will no longer cast a spell upon you; for you will know that person well at your first meeting. Conversely, you will form valuable friendships that you might otherwise have overlooked.

Acquaintanceship 

This subject is of peculiar importance. In our daily life we are constantly making acquaintances. This is particularly true of people who live in large cities.

These acquaintanceships may never become friendships. They may be chance meetings; formal introductions; contact with strangers. Yet they may become matters of great consequence. 

From such acquaintances you may sometimes form friendships. Take the man who joins an organization; he meets many persons who will become formal acquaintances thereafter: persons who will know him by name or reputation. 13

CHARACTER READING 

He should know what kind of people these folks are; if he considers it important to choose friends from among them, he should know which ones to choose.

Character Reading solves this difficulty. 

It is interesting to know that many prominent persons, people who appear in public, and whose acquaintanceship is esteemed, employ Character Analysis to a tremendous extent. But there are others who have no knowledge of this valuable science, and who suffer criticism as a result. 

The wise course is to be one of the group who can analyze and understand. It will prove profitable and useful.

Business Relationships 

Here is a subject quite as important as Acquaintanceship: so important, in fact, that it will be continued under separate headings.

Nearly all men—and a great many women-have important business relationships. Those who seek employment and who have choice of several places to work should certainly know the type of people with whom they are to come in contact. The girl who has a choice of a secretarial position in more than one office is confronted with a problem that may affect her future happiness. 

Pick the people with whom you are to work. How?

Through Character Reading. When you see opportunity, be sure that it will be congenial. No position is free from the human element. When you have made your choice, you will want to make the most of your opportunity. Character Analysis will enable you to do so.

Many persons are dissatisfied with their present jobs and are willing to seek new ones. Yet their real opportunity lies where they now are. They do not realize this, because they do not understand the people with whom they work. By employing Character 

Reading they can overcome perplexing difficulties and gain the harmony that they desire.

In business dealings, men sometimes make the mistake of extending credit or relying upon persons who do not deserve such trust. Sometimes they refuse a favor to a man who would return it ten-fold. This is because they are not good judges of character. 

With the power of Character Reading at their disposal, they will save themselves worry and money; and their business will improve. 14

Partnerships are also an important matter-in fact, the most important of all. When a man stakes his reputation upon the actions of another, he should be sure of the reliability of his associate. Here is where Character Reading has a vital use. Salesmanship

No one needs Character Reading more than the salesman. In fact the difference between a good salesman and a poor one is the ability to read character, or the lack of ability to read character. 

The man who can size up a prospective customer is sure to sell well. The "natural born" salesman is the one who has that intuitive understanding. Yet he will run second to the man who learns Character Analysis and applies it systematically. The 

"natural born" salesman may be a wonderful guesser; but after all, he is just a guesser. The salesman who does not guess, but who uses scientific knowledge will be one hundred per cent efficient.

Sales resistance is also important. Some men are known as "easy marks" who will buy anything. They have no sales resistance; they are dupes for swindlers and schemers. They say "No" to the sincere salesman because he does not promise enough, and they say "Yes" to the man who talks big but who does not mean what he says.

If such persons will study Character Analysis, their troubles will be at an end. They will know the sincere man the minute he walks into the office; they will detect the insincerity of the faker. 

Employment 

The greatest use of Character Analysis lies in employment.

In this field, the reputation of the individual is not all that is at stake; the welfare of a big corporation may depend upon the choice of the proper men for certain positions. 

Mere written qualifications-past records, and the like-are frequently deceiving. Very often a dozen or more applicants can show the same experience and can present recommendations of equal importance.

The right men must be chosen on their own merits. They must be studied quickly and efficiently by the person who does the employing. Through Character Reading he can make the correct choice. 

That is the reason why Character Analysis has been introduced into employment agencies; why it is used by industrial organizations. It is the sure method of making a final decision. 15

CHARACTER READING 

Every employer should use Character Analysis, and preach it to the men under him. Returning to the previous heading, every 

Sales Manager should insist that his salesmen master the subject. 

Covering the general subject of Business Relationships, every business man should possess an individual knowledge of Character Analysis. Today is an age of progress. All enterprises, great or small, are in competition with each other. Often they are equally matched. The one that utilizes Character Analysis will have the advantage over the other. Both should use it; all should use it, if they wish to be progressive; for only through scientific, systematic method may progress be obtained. 

GENERAL CHARACTERISTICS 

The first study may be called "personality at a distance"-the general impression that you might gain of such a person with only a fleeting glimpse.

We recognize that there are distinctive differences in people, with the potentiality of putting them into general groups without delay. The fact that the first of these groupings are obvious is quite important; for it is proof evident that Character Reading is a science.

From these comparatively simple divisions of general characteristics we will pass later to analyses that are not so easily recognizable, yet which are quite as important, once they are understood.

MASCULINE AND FEMININE 

Here are the first immediate classes: Male and Female. Our subject must belong to one of these groups; therefore he or she possesses certain general qualifications that are truly distinctive. 

Men and women are not superior or inferior to each otherthey are different. That is important. Modern anthropologists and students of human progress have said that the human family was once homogeneous. 

Whether or not that is correct, it is certain that civilization has forced a separation which has brought about traits that are entirely different in men and women. 

In the simple process of building the home, providing food and protection, and in raising families, certain tasks involved upon the males; and others were the lot of the females. 16

Problems of birth; the long period of infancy peculiar to mankind, and the comparatively slow development of the young made it essential for the woman to take care of the home, and to depend upon some form of shelter and protection; while to man came the work of providing; of exploring, hunting, fishing, and working constantly.

This led the males to become more agile and more audacious; more active, and more commanding. Their minds developed into creative channels; they gained mental alertness and physical ruggedness.

Whereas the females became more accustomed to comfort and ease; they gained gracefulness and relied upon intuition; their natures became peaceful and they were dependent-yet resourceful-with advisory ability.

Masculine Qualities 

The representative type of man shows distinct physical qualities: he is tall, broad-shouldered, and muscular. His hands and feet are large; his features are rugged. His facial expressions are those of serious thought, firmness, and perception. The coarser elements of human nature are predominant-mentally as well as physically. Hence Coarseness is a Masculine Quality.

The masculine mind is aggressive. It enjoys physical activity, rough sports, and does not mind minor injuries. The typical man is fond of leadership, and seeks adventure.

Man wants things of a practical nature: he is inventive, and cumbersome, heavy articles appeal to him-so long as they are useful. His pride in such implements as fire-arms, golf-clubs and base-ball bats is merely a survival of his old love of spears, swords and sling-shots.

His mind responds to direct, forceful suggestions. He can be impressed by tales of adventure. He will lay aside important matters for something that promises activity and virile interest. He likes to have his own way, and is liable to indulge in excesses, for he is not regardless of consequences when no great restraint or convincing argument to the contrary is offered. 

He is proud, and scornful of weakness. When met by temporary defeat, his desire for victory is increased. He will struggle against adverse circumstances.

Feminine Qualities 

The representative type of woman is a marked contrast to the representative type of man: she is short, with small figure 17

CHARACTER READING 

inclined to plumpness. Her hands and feet are small; her features are delicate and well-molded. Her facial expressions are happy, sympathetic, and tender. The finer elements of human nature are predominant. Hence Fineness is a Feminine Quality. 

The Feminine mind is not aggressive. It enjoys gentleness, avoids roughness, and suffers pain from slight injuries. The typical woman is dependent, and seeks security. 

Woman wants commodities that give personal comfort; she seeks articles of personal adornment. She loves delicacy, and likes jewelry, fine clothes, and ornaments which are not of practical use. Woman is adaptable rather than inventive.

Her mind is responsive to subtle suggestions. She is not easily diverted from a desire. She is submissive, yet wise enough to get what she wants; and she enjoys intrigue. She is cautious whenever her actions may endanger her position. ​Woman is vain, and generally ready to admit weakness. 

When she is really defeated, she becomes submissive, although she may secretly plan vengeance. She may pretend submissiveness in order to deceive; for she is constantly resourceful. She will yield to adverse circumstances, and seek to make the most of new conditions.

Mixed Types 

The same civilization which produced these differences in men and women has, in its progress, modified certain of these characteristics. Thus virile man has learned to appreciate finer things of life; he has become appreciative of comfort and delicacies. Effeminate woman, on the contrary, has learned to seek adventure; to indulge in sport, and to gain self-reliance. 

But in a crisis, the distinctive traits become apparent. Man is always ready to throw aside the luxuries that he has enjoyed and to adapt himself to severe conditions; while woman is always glad to return to her environment of ease and pleasant surroundings.

Instinctively the types are the same as always-Coarseness representing man; Fineness representing woman. Fundamentally their minds run in these opposite channels, and both men and women constantly betray their natural inclinations. 

The adoption of the opposite qualities is merely superficialcaused through the process of reasoning that desires a certain amount of mutual understanding; but human nature is not changed. 18

Thus the "Mixed Types" are not truly existent. They have come about purely because both the fully representative man and the completely representative woman belong to a semi-barbarous age. The "Mixed Types" are fundamentally Masculine and

Feminine; they have widened their fields, but have not crossed the dividing line. 

Opposite Types 

The "Feminine Man" and the "Masculine Woman" are distinct types, but their percentage is extremely small. Yet their characteristics are so obvious that they are easily identified, and therefore their actual number is exaggerated. 

The first class consists of men who have feminine instincts; the second group of women who have masculine instincts; and they should be judged accordingly.

Such types are not a product of modern civilization; but they flourish under present-day conditions because distinct masculinity and femininity are not compulsory as they were in the past. 

During the period of early civilization the "Feminine Man" could not have endured the hardships that he would have met; nor would the "Masculine Woman" have been tolerated, although her type was exemplified in the fables of the ancient Amazons. 

Summary of Masculine and Feminine 

These two types are important in any readings of character. 

No matter what an analysis may show, it must be remembered that the normal man has Masculine interests, while the normal woman has Feminine inclinations. 

This preliminary understanding is of essential value, for it serves as a basis in further study; and all traits must be interpreted with this reservation. The man who is of Motive Type will have that quality in Masculine affairs; the Motive Type woman will show the trait in Feminine activities.

COLOR CHARACTERISTICS 

Having determined whether our subject is masculine or feminine, another consideration is immediately discovered—that of race. 

There are three predominating colors: Black, Yellow, and

White, and it is commonly understood that these three types are quite different. 19

CHARACTER READING 

The Black Race is largely primitive, and its native state has, in the past, bordered on savagery. With civilization, this race has shown amazing progress, but it has largely been due to contact with the White Race; therefore it is difficult to analyze. 

Savage tribes do not possess a great deal of individuality; 

They must be studied chiefly as groups, although among the males there are usually persons of high ability in leadership. 

The marked traits of the Black Race are: 

Imitativeness: Clearly evident by its progress under civilization, with the acquiring of peculiarities of speech. 

Irresponsibility: A survival of old customs, gained from the environment of the tropics where food and shelter are easily obtained.

Loyalty: A trait that shows itself in the civilized individual, who will remain constant and faithful. 

The Yellow Race 

The characteristics of the Yellow Race are typically its own, and are too lengthy as well as too little understood to warrant a lengthy discussion here.

The Oriental mind—particularly that of the Yellow Raceis decidedly different from the Occidental mind, and its analysis is a subject by itself.

The White Race 

The White Race is composed of numerous nationalities. 

These differ in color from the extremely dark to the very light. 

It is through color-not through nationality-that the distinguishing characteristics are determined.

This forms an interesting and important development of the Masculine and Feminine characteristics previously described. The differences between light and dark persons are not nearly so obvious as the Masculine and Feminine qualities; yet they are quite as inherent, and the reader will probably be surprised at their importance. 

Blonde and Brunette

The lighter members of the Caucasian race are defined as "Blondes"; the darker members as "Brunettes." It is a fact that the Blondes are more frequent in the northern regions of the temperate zone, whereas the Brunettes inhabit the districts of warmer climate. 20

As a result, the Blondes have developed into more active, virile types; whereas the Brunettes have become languorous, and artistic. 

Explorations and hardy, primitive activities have been the part of the blonde races. They are noted for their war-like qualities; their desire to conquer and to rule. 

The Brunettes have been powerful as developers of civilization, with the luxuries and comforts pertaining thereto. 

It is said that the Norsemen discovered the continent of America long before Columbus; yet having discovered it, did nothing of consequence. This was partly due to their Blonde characteristics; yet more because of their primitive civilization. 

But when Columbus discovered America, he was somewhat disappointed. His purpose was not to find a continent, but to gain a shorter trade-route to wealthy India. 

The Spanish colonized America, but with definite ideas of gaining wealth and ease. Ponce-de-Leon sought the "Fountain of Youth"; other Spaniards gained the wealth of the civilized natives of Mexico and Peru. Their activities showed Brunette qualities.

The English colonized America, driving out the natives; they preyed upon Spanish trade, and were decidedly the aggressors in all their activities-showing the Blonde racial characteristics. 

The Blonde Type Physical characteristics: 

Light complexion. 

Light colored hair. 

Blue eyes.

It becomes evident that the Blonde type is distinctly Masculine. That is, it shows Masculine characteristics to the greatest degree. Virility, the Masculine trait, is predominant. 

Blonde Men are aggressive, usually large of build (on the average) and quick to overcome obstacles. They enjoy travel, and adventure; are capable to leadership, and apt to go to extremes in dissipation. 

They work speedily, and with enthusiasm; they will fight against odds, and will often overcome them. They like to see life and to experience variety. They are athletic and recuperate quickly, after heavy labor. They are fond of outdoor life and can withstand hardship. They are very courageous and are restless and ambitious. They gain their results by direct methods. 21

CHARACTER READING 

They understand a situation quickly, and swing to action without hesitation. Yet they are prone to gain superficial impressions. They form likes and dislikes and are quick to show them. 

They enter into enterprises hastily, and are often found in illconsidered schemes.

Blonde Women have certain of these qualities. While they are, of course, Feminine by nature, they show the Blonde traits. 

They are excitable, aggressive, and easily betray their emotions. They crave excitement, and obtain it when they want it. 

They have a marked influence over men, because they can understand the Masculine traits.

They stand up for their rights, and make no effort to conceal their likes and dislikes. They demand whatever they desire, and will not yield to any marked degree. Once they have set themselves upon a definite idea, they will make no change. They become indignant very easily, and will end a discussion abruptly. 

They are often talkative, and will change subjects rapidly. 

If they do not understand a subject they will cast it aside as being of small consequence. They do not, as a rule, distinguish between matters of importance and those of lesser consequence. 

They gain impressions rapidly, and find out a great deal, but do not apply knowledge extensively. They have the Feminine desire for jewelry and fine clothes, but this is more because they love to possess, rather than because of great delight in the articles themselves.

They are disregardless of cost, when they can afford much; but they are careful and wise when necessary. They can be extremely economical, when they so desire. They are resourceful and when one plan does not succeed, they turn quickly to another. 

The Brunette Type Physical characteristics: 

Dark complexion. 

Dark brown (or black) hair. 

Brown eyes. 

The Brunette type is Feminine; in the men, the Masculine traits are less evident than in the Blonde type; in the women, the 

Feminine characteristics are predominant. 

Brunette Men are not usually so large, so quick or so aggressive as the Blonde type. They are more used to meditation and deliberation. They consider a matter before undertaking it, and seldom proceed unless they feel sure of success. 22

They are cautious and conservative-active and distinctly Masculine when the occasion calls, but with a natural tendency to avoid conflict.

They are philosophical and religious, but are also inclined to pessimism. They do not have the recuperative powers found in the Blonde, but they are patient, steady and enduring. 

Brunette men enjoy luxury, and are not anxious to give it up. 

They are not easily excited; but they resent injustices and will prove revengeful. They are active when they need to be; but they are subtle in their ways, and accomplish as much as they can before they spring to action.

They take losses very much to heart, and need encouragement to attempt new activity; but when they decide to exert themselves, they show great power.

They do not grasp everything immediately, but they have a keen human understanding and see deeply into matters which men of the Blonde type may treat superficially. 

Brunette Women portray the purely feminine. They are thoughtful, and can conceal their emotions. They desire ease, and love inactivity.

They can be extremely friendly to those whom they dislike, and can conceal their actual attitudes toward people. They obtain their desires by artfulness, and their love can turn to hate. They do not show indignation quickly, and they have a fine sense of distinction.

They are patient, and will harbor a secret desire or dislike for a long time, waiting until the proper moment to express it. They love gems and ornaments because of the beauty of such articles. 

Women of the Brunette type will cherish articles of comparatively small value-often in preference to expensive things. They can appreciate beautiful objects whether they own them or not.

They have excellent taste; they are sentimental and think much of old associations.

While not excessive, they are often extravagant to a degree; and their understanding of men is entirely different from that of the Blonde. Many Brunettes do not understand men well; those who do exert the superior Feminine traits to conquer the weaknesses of men.

Brunettes possess gracefulness, and artistic ability. The true 

Brunette is quiet, observant, and meditative; when aroused to anger women of this type are dangerous and vengeful. 23

CHARACTER READING Analysis of Contrast 

There are few races of the extreme Blonde type existent today, for civilization has tempered their war-like spirits. The Danish invaders of Britain are representative of this type, with their hardiness, desire for conflict, and disdain of weakness.

The extreme Brunette type is seen in the Hindus-who are governed by a complex mysterious religion; who are languorous and fond of ease; who have caste systems that are more important than the valor of the individual. The stoicism of these people is gained through meditation and high learning.

The Blonde races of Northern Europe are typical of the normal Blonde; the Brunette races of Southern Europe represent the normal Brunette.

The Middle Type 

A great many people-particluarly in America—represent a 

Middle Type, between the Blonde and the Brunette. They have the harmonized qualities of each. In analyzing individuals, color is of value when the subject has definite tendencies toward one of the two types: Blonde or Brunette. Auburn hair and freckled faces are inclined toward the Blonde type. 

The "Middle Type" or Balance has light brown hair; complexion neither dark nor light; eyes green, gray, or mixed. When features depart from these the result is generally a definite inclination to Blonde or Brunette.

The Mixed Type 

Persons with both outstanding traits of Blondes and Brunettes form a "Mixed Type" which must be studied according to which seems to be most predominant. They will never be typical 

Blondes or Brunettes, and certain qualities of the opposite type may appear in them in a marked degree. 

FURTHER CHARACTERISTICS 

Flesh

Hard Flesh, of tough fibre, shows harsh, unyielding characteristics. It is the coarseness of Masculine tendency.

It shows stubbornness, firmness of opinion, lack of emotion, and lack of sympathy. Individuals who possess this characteristic are abrupt in action; they are often efficient and conscientous; and they do not yield to sentiment. 24

You can tell them from a hand-shake; their grip is generally as firm as their flesh. They are usually economical, and, whether male or female, will be appreciative of anything that is useful and durable.

Hardness is an inherent quality in them. They are hard workers; hard drivers; and hard thinkers, who will not stand for idleness or waste.

They are determined in action, and have resistance and selfconfidence, with the courage of their own convictions.

No matter whether they are Blonde or Brunette, this characteristic of firmness will be evident. They may have either the aggressiveness of the Blonde or the subtlety of the Brunette; that is immaterial. They will not yield, nor give up their determination.

The Blonde, with Hard Flesh as a characteristic will usually be exceptionally virile; the Brunette with such traits will show extreme power even though his tendencies may be toward artistic efforts.

Soft Flesh is a sign of indulgence. People who have this characteristic are easily pleased and will listen to persuasion. They usually have supple, pliable bodies; and their minds are likewise. 

They are friendly, and ready to change their opinions. 

They have, therefore, a persuasive ability; and while they are not drivers they are good workers, and can gain popularity among those whom they control.

They are not as immune to hardships as persons of the Hard Flesh type, and they are apt to be spendthrifts. They appreciate ingenuity, and are not overly concerned with the practicability or usefulness of anything, as they prefer novelty.

The Blonde with the Soft Flesh is the type who gives way to excess, easily led from a definite purpose and ready to experiment with the unusual. The Brunette with such traits will accentuate his love for ease and will usually prefer idleness to effort.

Elastic Flesh does not have the hardness of Hard Flesh, but is not of the flabby, Soft Flesh feel. It gives the impression of smoothness or softness, but with a marked stability and firmness. 

This is the average flesh consistency. It is the intermediate degree; and indicates the individual who is energetic, and ready to work, yet who is fond of pleasure and does not look upon it as a folly. 25

CHARACTER READING 

It indicates the type who puts everything in its proper place; who takes an interest in amusements and trivial matters and becomes enthusiastic, yet who will not allow such indulgences to interfere with business.

Texture 

Coarseness or Fineness of skin texture is another factor in the general analysis of the individual. 

They show the traits of Masculine and Feminine. The representative man, of virile active type, would be expected to have skin of coarse texture; while the representative woman would have skin of fine texture.

It cannot be said that texture plays an extremely important part in general Character Reading; yet it must not be overlooked, for it gives a real insight into the likes and inclinations of the individual.

Coarse Texture of skin shows a tendency toward natural life, with little appreciation of artificialities. People whose skin is rough, large-pored, and generally coarse have no dislike for manual labor, and are fond of making things.

Whether or not they are capable of great physical energy, they will have a love of nature, and will be satisfied in rather primitive surroundings. 

It is a noteworthy fact that many people who have money and can enjoy every luxury are fond of "roughing it"; while others who do outdoor work seek comfort. This is not due so much to a desire for change as to natural inclination.

Rough skin texture is one of the characteristics of the person who delights in going back to nature. He may have fine tastes, and love the higher things of life; yet the call of the outdoorsthe ruggedness of life-will have its appeal. 

We know that many laborers have rough hands, and we attribute this to toil; but careful study will show that the roughness and coarseness of skin was always evident. Every person, at an early age, undertakes something that requires manual work. 

Those who have coarse skin experience no difficulty and are encouraged to continue; whereas fine, tender skin causes difficulty and brings discouragement.

Coarse skin gives us an inkling of instinctive likes. People who have this physical feature are not delicate and do not mind the coarseness of life. They can adapt themselves to rough, painful work, without minding it. 26

A person of rough skin texture will not appreciate certain fine things; nor will such a person worry because of trivial differences in materials.

To them a book is a book-whether bound in cloth or in limp-leather; rough furnishings are quite as acceptable as smooth, polished mahogany.

Fine Texture, distinguished by velvety skin, and almost invisible pores, denotes a person of sensitive nature, more appreciative of art than nature.

People of this type do not enjoy the rougher elements. Thunder storms disturb them, instead of awakening their interest-as in the case of those with coarse skin texture. 

Being of fine quality, as evidenced by their skin, such people do not enjoy extremes of weather. They prefer to remain indoors during a blizzard, while those of rough texture will choose to brave the snow-storm.

They appreciate nature, but do not like to go about in the rain, or to experience hot weather. The temperate condition suits them perfectly.

Whether or not they are cultured; no matter what their occupations may be, they will always like the nicer things of life and will turn against anything that is coarse.

Skin texture explains this very readily. Some people cannot endure the feel of a flannel shirt, because their skins are fine. They are not suited to "roughing it"; manual labor causes them pain and inconvenience, and they shun it.

These natural protective tendencies lead directly to appreciation of the finer things of life. The mental activities of the person with fine skin texture fits in with his physical feelings. His nature becomes sensitive through a very natural process; he will show a preference for politeness and fine manners.

Many war workers at ship-yards, who took up that occupation not through desire but because of high wages, spent their earnings on silk shirts, fine clothes and other luxuries. They were persons of fine skin texture.

Summary of Skin Texture 

The headings that follow should be studied in conjunction with skin texture as they deal with subjects very much akin. They show still more clearly that coarseness and fineness are ingrained qualities, quite apart from other characteristics. 27

CHARACTER READING 

While Coarseness is distinctly Masculine, there are many virile active men, who, because of Fineness of skin texture, are not fond of coarse things. Similarly, Fineness, while Feminine, may not be found in all women.

Quite apart from sex and color, certain people are endowed with coarse or fine skins, and their instinctive adaptability causes them to show definite inclinations.

As contrast, the person of fine skin texture will bathe more often than necessary, to gain an imaginary feeling of perfect cleanliness, while a person of coarse skin texture will perform ablutions merely bcause they are necessary. 

The person of fine skin texture will dislike vulgarity; a person of coarse skin texture will not mind it. 

These differences will prove very evident when two persons of distinctly opposite skin texture are analyzed. But with most individuals, coarse or fine skin texture are not exaggerated; hence their peculiar traits or susceptibilities are not present to a full degree. Such factors are evident, but are normally modified. 

Hair Structure 

The color of hair has been discussed under Blondes and Brunettes. The structure of the hair is a quality that goes with skin texture. Usually persons of coarse skin type have rough, or bristling hair-of coarse quality. This is an element that should be considered as a corollary to skin texture. 

Fine, silky, smooth hair goes with fineness of skin texture.

Very often it is easier to analyze a person for coarseness or fineness by considering the hair before the skin texture. 

Curly, wavy hair generally indicates fineness, unless the hair is notably coarse. A great number of talented persons have fine wavy or curly hair. 

Coarse hair is associated with roughness; like coarse skin texture, it indicates the matter-of-fact individual; while fine hair is associated with softness, and indicates a sensitive disposition. 

Facial Features

Facial features, taken generally, with no specific attempt at further analysis, will often show Coarseness or Fineness.

Features which are large, or over-developed, usually are accompanied by coarse skin texture, and accentuate the qualities of that type. 28

Fine, delicately molded meatures are indicative of fine skin texture and are almost invariably coincident with that quality. 

They show the sensitive disposition, wtih its love of fineness. 

Where these three elements are all present: Coarse Skin; 

Rough Hair; Large Features-it will be a sure sign of Coarseness, and the presence of the qualities described under the heading of Coarse Texture. Unless the three are present to some degree, the subject will not be a distinctive member of that type. 

The presence of the opposite elements: Velvety Skin; Soft 

Hair; Delicate Features-is positive evidence of Fineness and the existence to a marked degree of the qualities listed under Fine 

Texture. 

These three qualities are all needed in order to portray the distinctive member of the Fine Texture Type.

FORMATION 

Proceeding from the study of General Characteristics, with its distant view, we come to the more specific study of Formation -the science of telling characteristics by the shape of the head and face.

There are four distinct phases of this study; the first and third being the most important. They are: 

(1) Full Front Formation. 

(2) Back Head Formation. 

(3) Side Head Formation. 

(4) Profile Formation. 

Besides these, there are other factors in formation that also assist in the analysis of the individual; but with the three named the ground-work is obtained. 

Formation is the first distinguishing step in Character Analysis; and it should be regarded as of great importance. Besides the study of the face and head formation, attention should be given to the general shape of the body-not as a separate phase of 

Analysis, but as a corollary to head and face formation. Certain types of bodies are clues to correct judgment of Full Front Formation.

FULL FRONT FORMATION 

The first impression that one gains of a person is a full-front view of the face and head. Imagine the face to be quite blank, for we are not yet considering its features or its expressions. 29

CHARACTER READING 

Here the unscientific judge of character makes his mistake. So centered is he on minor features that he pays no attention to formation. Yet the shape of the head and face is of prime importance. 

​Vital Type Motive Type Mental Type 

Heads and faces are of three shapes: Triangular, Square, and 

Round. Each of these represents a distinctive type of person.

Many persons combine qualities of different types; but the three types are fundamental, and you will frequently encounter people who are typical examples of one of these three groups. 

Triangular Form-Mental Type 

The Triangular Form of head and face is easily recognized. 

The head has the greatest width at the top. It narrows downward, toward the jaw, and ends in a narrow chin. 

Allowing, of course, for the natural curve of the head, it forms a rough triangle, the top side horizontal, for the top of the head; the other two sides like a letter V, meeting beneath the chin. 

This is the distinctive Mental Type.

Just as the top of the head is larger than the bottom, so does the mind have the greatest function in this type of person. 

These people are constant thinkers. They are studious, and fond of learning. They are quick to receive mental impressions. 

They are intellectual and inclined to be idealistic. 

Mental Type persons are thinkers and originators. They place physical exertion as secondary to mental development. 

They are suited for inside work; they handle difficult mental problems quickly, easily, and naturally. 

They have excellent memories, and their ability to reason and obtain results through reasoning is often a mystery to other persons. 30

Psychologists speak of "virtuosos"-persons whose ability in certain kinds of mentality is far beyond the normal curve of intelligence rating. Such people are almost invariably of the 

Mental Type. 

They have the ability to think deeply without effort; and their mental endurance is amazing. Their minds are constantly active, and are not easily subject to strain, unless they ignore rest periods, and overtax themselves without realizing it. 

People of this type will lose themselves in their work; will forget the passing of time; and will become irregular in their habits. Once they start something, they gain enthusiasm and keep at it.

They are keen and analytical; ready to undertake anything that requires thinking; ready to match their efforts against those of others. Once they are settled in a direct course, they will make tremendous progress. Their interest centers in their effort; not in the reward. They are inventive because they are ingenious; not because they want to make money easily.

They are constantly devising ways to interest their minds; they are quick to take up something new and to work upon it; and their brains are constantly planning improvements. 

The Mental Type is also nervous, and therefore, inclined to be irritable, especially when something disturbs the course of mental activity. These are natural traits which are often accentuated by over-activity in brain-work. 

The person of the Mental Type may become absent-minded; oblivious to other things in the interest of the present. Yet they are precise and clear and love to use their observation in study. 

The great fault of the Mental Type is lack of physical activity. As a rule the extreme Mental person is not physically strong. 

Slender build is usual. Interest lying in the mind, the body is forgotten. High pressure mental activity is more important than physical welfare.

The Proper Development of the Mental Type 

This requires careful thought and real attention. The Mental person should seek education; for he has the aptitude of using all his knowledge. He should also impress upon himself the importance of a good physique, and should take enforced periods of rest. 

Concentrated effort is essential to real happiness and progress in the Mental Type. Scattered interests lead to divided strength of purpose, and injure the real efficiency found in this type. 31

CHARACTER READING 

Once entered upon an active purpose, regularity should be employed. The tendency to work for extra hours; to work at any time; to neglect meals and sleep are injurious to the physical being and will undermine the mental power of the individual. 

Men of the Mental Type 

Men of the Mental Type are constant workers, and are much more apt to experience difficulties than are women of this type. 

First, because they go to extremes of action when they have found the work they like. Second, because they try to do too much and to conduct too many side-lines or varied enterprises. Third, because they can become despondent when they are unsuccessful, and will feel resentful at the success of others. 

Their line of least resistance is to do the things they want to do. They avoid certain details, not because they cannot do them but because they do not care to handle them. Thus they are powerful in executive capacity, because they can plan, originate, and suggest, leaving the details and accomplishment to other persons.

Women of the Mental Type 

Women of the Mental Type have great opportunity to enter the occupational field. They can succeed in many enterprises where men are found.

They will not be happy unless they are using mental effort. 

Like the men of this type, they can suffer from over-exertion -not because they overtax their minds, but because they do more than they are physically capable of performing. 

They are so sensitive that they may experience nervous breakdowns through over-activity. Their best plan is to find some definite field of endeavor that will satisfy all their desire for mental effort, and concentrate upon it, with discretion. 

Children of the Mental Type

The education of children of this type is important. They are quick, alert, and learn easily; therefore they should be encouraged to gain all the schooling possible.

If they are led to work toward some profession, or have a definite purpose ahead, they will devote great time to their schoolwork; otherwise they will neglect it for extra-curriculum learning. They are apt to be constant readers, and they will obtain knowledge whether or not they have education; but unless that knowledge is systematic, it will not help them, for it will have 32

nothing to do with the proper development of the Mental Type -namely, concentration.

Faults of the Mental Type 

Every type has its faults and those of the Mental Type are peculiarly dangerous. First, they are sometimes neglectful of things which they have promised to do. This is not through inability, but through lack of interest. They can best be encouraged by a definite promise of something more when they have finished what they are first given. If they like work, they want to do it constantly. That is why they are well suited to professions. 

Remember that their minds are constantly active. They are planning, all the time. They cannot be mentally idle. When they have some acquired knowledge or education, and can put it to use,, they are in good order; but if they have no ambition or can see none, they will turn to almost anything.

Many clever criminals are of the Mental Type. They will plan to swindle; they will take advantage of their friends; they will do many illegitimate things, from picking pockets to making counterfeit money.

The Mental Type person appreciates beauty and is idealistic; these people admire fine things and good institutions; but they must keep themselves active. Their minds can become warped and they can begin to admire crookedness as a form of beauty. It is all a matter of view-point, and the Mental Type person is an extremist. 

The vast majority of the type are reliable, trustworthy, and conscientious; the others are quite the opposite. There is no in between. The Mental Type person may occasionally give way to dishonesty, but he will regret it, and fight against it-unless he casts honesty and idealism overboard and then he will stop at nothing.

It is therefore evident that of all persons whose appearances may be deceitful, those are most likely to be of the Mental Type. 

Persuasive talkers, talented persons, human beings of excellent manners who turn out to be unreliable can be detected through 

Character Analysis. Anyone of the extreme Mental Type should be carefully studied, bearing in mind that that person is either thoroughly trustworthy or subtly dishonest. The great percentage are of the former class; but their opposites must not be ignored. 33

CHARACTER READING 

Occupations for the Mental Type

Professional work of all kinds is particularly suited to persons of the Mental Type. They are successful as pharmacists, mathematicians (accountants), editors, proofreaders, designers, and particularly as instructors, professors, teachers, and educators, as well as librarians.

They are suited to any field where mental alertness is important; and they can handle complicated detail work, and jobs where memory is important. But they are not suited for ordinary routine. Their field is specialized effort. 

Square Form-Motive Type 

The Square or Rectangular Form is easy to recognize. The head is about the same width at the top as at the jaws; and the jaws are heavy, giving the thought of power. 

The side lines of the face are almost parallel, and we can picture a head of this type as fitting with an elongated square or rectangle, allowing for the top curve of the head, and the curve of the chin.

In addition to the head formation, the body also gives a clue to the Square Form. It is not thin or light in weight, as in the 

Triangular Form, but is heavy and stalwart, built squarely, like the head.

Some persons of this form are quite tall; others quite short; but the same similarity of formation is present in both. 

The Square Form of the face and head indicates the Motive Type of person; people who are fond of action and competition; constantly in motion and physical activity. 

Will power and independence are found in persons of the Motive Type. They are pioneers and leaders in many activities. 

They are noted for their constructive efforts. 

There is determination in persons of the Motive Type. They are thinkers, but they think in terms of action. They are not content with directing, but are ready to do things themselves. In fact, they are the type of people who will insist that "the only way to get anything done is to do it yourself." 

This is largely because they expect other people to show the same action that they display. They are not contented with halfway measures or long effort; they want to force the issue and get results. 34

People of this type are set in their opinions. They are unyielding and cannot be coerced. They will listen to reason and coaxing on the part of persons whom they trust; but they cannot be driven.

Just as those of the Mental Type often labor long and think deeply without thought of great compensation, so do people of the Motive Type spring into action for the joy of action alone. 

They stand out for what they believe are their rights, and will champion the cause of those who seem to be oppressed. 

They become dissatisfied and restless when they are restricted. 

They dislike confinement or smallness. They want to learn by experience, rather than by study; and when they are in school athletics are more important to them than the class-rooms. 

Those of the Motive Type are capable of heavy work. They have natural physical qualifications, and strengthen them through bodily development, which they regard as of first import. 

Once they have learned a calling or vocation, they stick to it, and think in terms of that alone. Hence they rise to high positions through forcefulness, and constancy, and using every bit of thought for one purpose.

This gives them value in professions, for while they do not have great aptitude for study, they are convincing in their methods. Thus a man of the Motive Type would make an excellent plea as a lawyer; he would establish his case convincingly; but as a person to consult he would not compare with a lawyer of the Mental Type. 

Persons of the Motive Type are firm under fire. They are powerful in emergencies. They are not subject to spells of nervousness. They have self-confidence.

The Proper Development of the Motive Type 

Persons of the Motive Type must not let their spirits carry them away. They must seek to develop forethought and to overcome prejudice and hasty action.

They should seek mental stimulus, and endeavor to keep in contact with subjects other than those in their particular field. 

They already have concentrated effort, but it may be too concentrated. Their proper course is to expand, and extend their influence. Those who have done so have become great personages. 

They should seek to use the advice of others, and learn the habit of contemplation. They need broad-mindedness and a more human understanding. 35

CHARACTER READING Men of the Motive Type 

Men of the Motive Type become great leaders, because of their ability to force an issue and to act promptly and effectively. But they can often make mistakes, and lay themselves open to criticism or defeat.

They may waste their time attending to minor details. The manufacturer who angrily steps out of the office and shows men how to unload a truck is typical of the Motive person. 

Yet this same desire for action often enables such a man to gain the respect of his associates through his willingness to undertake anything.

Men of the Motive Type have the determination to do; they will often undertake something which seems impossible, and will carry it to a successful conclusion. They are physically enduring, capable of achievement, and at their best in work where physical activity is paramount.

Women of the Motive Type 

Here are the women who go in for outdoor sports; aviation; and adventure-with success. They are not planners or organizers; they are doers.

They have faith in their own ability, and can accomplish many things that cannot be equalled by men. 

They are strong adherents of women's rights. Women of the 

Motive Type were prominent in the Suffrage movement. They are capable, active and will not tolerate shiftless persons. 

They often manage to get themselves disliked or misunderstood, but they do not care. They are not necessarily of a Masculine Type; in fact the majority of them are decidedly Feminine in characteristics. But they crave action; will rule the home if the husband is incompetent; can support the family when occasion demands; and are not content to deal in trvialities. 

Children of the Motive Type 

Children of the Motive Type are often a great source of difficulty. They cannot be forced, and will become obstinate if ordered to do things. They have a great idea of their own independence. They are usually leaders among their companions, and love to participate in outdoor sports.

They must be urged to progress, and must not be dealt with too harshly. An appeal to their pride; to their desire for achieve- 36

ment; and similar courses are the best methods by which to direct them.

They respond to arguments that show immediate return for their good behaviour. Telling them to "think of their future, and talking of long-delayed success is useless. They want to see immediate return for the investment of desirable conduct, and they will show a determination if they are correctly handled. 

Faults of the Motive Type 

A great fault of persons of the Motive Type is their overreliance on energy without forethought. People of this type have the habit of belittling the ideas of others, particularly those of people of other types.

They carry their independence to extremes, giving way to foolish ideas, and ignoring information that is really useful to them. They attack too soon; they depend upon strength and purpose when they should use wisdom and discretion.

They are firm in their convictions, and do not know when they are stubborn or obstinate. They stand for principles without reasoning whether the principles are right or wrong; and they often injure their own interests through constant adherence to ideas which are unsound.

They often take a challenging attitude, prepared to argue and attack anything that is proposed to them. They lack tactfulness and become annoyed at anything which stands in their path. 

They applaud Alexander because he cut the Gordian knot instead of untying it, and they think that the same principle can be applied to anything and everything. They demand, and often get what they want; but they often lose out when they could gain something by diplomacy.

People of the Motive Type are not subtle; they are direct in method, and they express the opinions that they hold. Hence there is nothing deceptive about them. If they turn to bad pursuits or criminal practices, they use forceful methods, and are easily distinguished. It is not difficult to analyze the thoughts or plans of a person of this type. 

The Motive Type, therefore, is easy to understand; and you can judge the calibre of people of this type. But to persuade them or to gain their full confidence is not an easy matter. Their methods and their purposes are rock-bound, and when they exert their wills, they are difficult to oppose. Even their best friends cannot control them. 37

CHARACTER READING Occupations for the Motive Type 

Virile activities are for the Motive Type. They make excellent engineers, builders, mechanics, aviators, and managers. They are suited to certain forms of professional work. They are good railroad men; and some of them become politicians.

As statesmen, soldiers, or police officials, these men are powerful. They can find a place in any line of endeavor where power and purpose are necessary. They become explorers, and seamen. 

They are the type who can work from the bottom up, as they have a capacity for practical knowledge, and learn much through experience.

Circular Form-Vital Type 

The round face, circular, or slightly oval in form is the third group that is easily distinguished and which is independent of the others.

This represents the Vital Type: people whose temperament is easy-going; those who love life, and are neither eccentric-like the Mental Type-nor inclined to anger-like the Motive Type.

People with circular faces usually have rotund bodies-a sure sign of the Vital Type. They love comfort, have good practical knowledge of values, and know how to enjoy life. 

They seek health, wealth and happiness. They love good food and clothing and seek constant entertainment. They have an easy way of controlling people, and know how to make others work for them.

They are the promoters and speculators of commercial enterprises. They are willing to take chances; they are fond of good times, and like to see other people enjoy themselves also. 

They are seen as political bosses because they have a persuasive ability that gets results. They are always ready to help a friend; and even though their views may sometimes be unorthodox, they always have a sense of justice. 

Most persons of the Vital Type are of good humor. They enjoy a laugh, and are instinctively jolly. The majority of fat men come in this class, although corpulence is not necessary to a member of the Vital Type group. They are self-indulgent, but have excellent control of themselves, and seldom yield to excess. 

Where the person of the Mental Type may dissipate because of nervous tendencies, and a person of the Motive Type do the same through self-will, the man or woman of the Vital Type will be satisfied with a sufficiency. 38

While they are not close with money, nevertheless people of the Vital Type are cumulative, and have the ability to gain wealth. They know the value of money and have the faculty of entering lines of business where money is to be made. 

They have the aptitude of getting ideas from persons of the Mental Type and turning them over to those of the Motive Type, thus profiting from both. They are shrewd buyers, and good salesmen.

They are frank, friendly, and practical-minded. They are not particularly interested in scientific research or study, nor are they active. Their purpose is to establish themselves and thus gain the comfort and security that they desire; and these can be summed up generally as physical comfort or financial profit. 

People of the Vital Type have the ability to harbor their resources; they are recuperative after a loss largely because they have already planned some alternative method or have stored away something for the emergency. Furthermore, their method of bestowing friendship and favors is reciprocative; they generally receive a return for what they do, even though they do not demand immediate accounting.

Being enjoyers of life, and wise enough to enjoy life sensibly, those who have the Vital Type temperament are by no means lacking in capabilities. They often deny themselves certain pleasures, and lead very strict lives, in order to delight in indulgences which are harmless. They do not worry a great deal, nor do they take life seriously, yet they are wise and tactful. 

They seek to fulfill their desires, and will exert themselves to attain the positions that they would like to gain. But they are content with slow progress, and are always accumulating-plodding onward. 

Those of the Mental Type may forget their most important interests because of enthusiasm: Motive Type subjects may demand immediate action and fail to get it; but the Vital Type is patient and abiding, content in the knowledge that those who wait achieve their desires, if they do not wait idly.

The Proper Development of the Vital Type 

The Vital Type is almost self-developing; therefore it needs impetus. With luck and unexpected fortune, a person of this type will gain his full development with no effort; but for one who gets such results there will be many who never gain more than slight success. 39

CHARACTER READING 

People of the Vital Type should use their natural ability to its greatest extent; they should act even though conservatively -when opportunity presents itself. Those who have learned this lesson are the ones who have made the greatest success. 

Those of the Vital Type appreciate the good qualities of both the Mental and the Motive Type, and they should profit thereby -not merely by employing other persons to advantage, but by acquiring some of the tactics that the others use. 

The sense of discrimination of the Vital Type enables a person of this class to choose what is useful and valuable; and with thought and effort he can increase his own ability to a remarkable degree.

Men of the Vital Type 

Men of the Vital Type are reliable and persuasive. They give common-sense advice, and have excellent commercial instincts. 

They help other people, and thus help themselves. They are not easily aroused, and do not give way to anger. 

When in positions of importance, they become fixtures, and have a large part to play in any enterprise with which they are connected. They seldom become enthusiastic, and behave conservatively. While they protect their own interests, they are not governed by cupidity, and are often generous in their proposals. 

When they are associated in an enterprise in which money is lost, they generally lose very little; yet they do not receive great condemnation. This is because their policies are generally well understood from the beginning. 

They express a quiet confidence, and employ good judgment. 

They make their wants understood, and do not deviate. They enjoy good surroundings; when in business, they are particular about their offices and furnishings; and they like the best of everything.

Women of the Vital Type 

Women of the Vital Type are fond of home-life and fit in easily with their surroundings. They make economical and sensible house-wives although they take pride in the home and provide many comforts.

They are companionable and agreeable; they are good cooks and their natural forte is living happily and amiably. 

They are inclined to become careless, however, and to let domestic matters slide along. This is because their field, if limited, does not give them the same contact with life that men of 40

the Vital Type enjoy. These women are jovial, friendly, sympathetic and neighborly; but they are apt to be too talkative and too concerned with trivialities. If their homes are comfortable, they will be contented, and will not crave travel or excitement unless they acquire such a taste.

Children of the Vital Type 

Children of the Vital Type are not trouble-makers; nor are they studious and painstaking. They are quite easy to handle, and are not willful. But they are inclined to laziness and indifference, and can be spoiled by indulgence. 

They desire a great many things, and will become conceited and shiftless if they get everything they want. They will persistently ask for something and will annoy their parents continually if they are not curbed. The obtaining of one desire merely makes them want more; and therefore discipline is essential in handling them.

They do not respond to reasoning, as do children of the 

Mental Type; nor can their pride in achievement be reached, as with those of the Motive Type. A child of the Vital Type must be governed by definite rules and methods, and taught to know its privileges and its restrictions. Obedience is a natural quality of such a child, and parents should realize this. 

Faults of the Vital Type 

In the Vital Type, faults are of omission rather than of commission. There is a natural tendency here toward laziness, and the peculiar selfishness that can come from the over-developed ego. 

Action is frequently lacking; worry is seldom present. People of this type can accustom themselves to conditions which those of the Mental or Motive Types would not tolerate. They can lose themselves in pleasure and self-indulgence, and forget all purpose in life.

If they cannot get what they desire, they may follow the line of least resistance, contenting themselves with some lesser desire, and they can be lulled into self-satisfaction. They enjoy privileges and if their idea of self-importance is realized, they will make no further advance.

Such qualities may be constantly observed in people of the 

Vital Type. If they have lost fame or position, they make no effort to recover their former status- -so long as their present position is not undesirable. They are not ambitious, and when they 41

CHARACTER READING 

have good fortune, they measure it by the pleasure that it brings; hence if those pleasures are still obtainable, they do not mind losing some of the power that brought them.

Success merely paves the way to their desire; it does not encourage them to any achievement. At the same time, their progress being slow, steady, and patient, they often reach a position where they are secure and continue to advance without effort. 

People of the Vital type are not particularly worried about public opinion. They lack the conscientious scruples of the Mental Type; they are not filled with the spirit of determination that rules the Motive Type, with its pride of success and its scornfulness of failure. 

They carefully consider consequences, however, and will not take a risk unless they are sure the profit will be very great. They are not willing to sacrifice self-comfort if they can possibly avoid it.

Occupations for the Vital Type 

There being nothing inventive, constructive or dynamic in the Vital Type, the suitable occupations are those which lead to pleasant surroundings, and allow development of the cumulative powers so evident in the Vital Type.

These people fit in with work that has been long established; where friendliness, diplomacy and a fair amount of attention are useful. 

They are not students, and therefore good teachers or professors; neither are they highly active and suited to outdoor enterprises; but they have the peculiar aptitude of fitting into lines of work that are already organized, and gaining great attainment thereby. 

They are excellent merchants and business men. They do well as bankers, and stock-brokers. As treasurers of large institutions they are capable and will cause the funds to accumulate. 

They have considerable ability-if they develop it—as professional men. They are wonderful politicians; and when they attain political offices they are capable-particularly as magistrates and judges.

They do well in smaller jobs; can handle clerical positions, and do inside work; they are often the best of salesmen, because of their friendliness; and they become capable financiers. 

Trained to mechanical lines, they experience fair success, especially when they observe that their work is bringing them comforts that they desire. 42

Summary of the Three Types The Mental Type: The governing factor is REASON. Men tal process is regarded most highly. Constant thought; logicwhether correct or incorrect-dominates the individual.

The Motive Type: The governing factor is WILL. Physi cal strength is esteemed. Action is held as the quality that brings results. Determination is a power.

The Vital Type: The governing factor is DESIRE. Com fort, ease, and the joys of life ar placed above all else. The fulfillment of desire brings happiness.

Mixed Types 

In the analysis of Head and Façe Formation, one can read character easily in individuals who are distinctly Mental, Motive, or Vital.

But a great majority of persons exhibit combined formations of the Triangular, Square, or Round.

Hence the hybrid, or combined types are important to study, and here the Character Analyst will find that careful consideration is important. 

There are the Mental-Motive; the Mental-Vital; Motive 

Vital Types and these are quite as important as the three distinct types. Obviously they combine the characteristics described in the previous sections; but in order to give a clear understanding of their head and face formations and their natural traits, we shall consider each of the three combined types separately. 

Triangular-Square Formation: Mental-Motive Type 

In formation, the upper part of the head and general face formation give a Triangular appearance, but with a definite presence of the square jaw that marks the Motive Type. The tapering of the face on its downward contours is not so pronounced as in the Triangular Form, but it is evident.

The body, too, shows indications. While the pure Motive 

Type is usually firm set-whether tall or short-the Mental- 

Motive inclines toward slenderness. This type has a good physique; but is often sound in build, rather than husky. 

The true Mental-Motive person is a thinker who likes to put his plans into action. He creates and promotes. He needs exercise; unlike the pure Mental he cannot exist for long periods without physical activity. 43

CHARACTER READING 

The athlete who ranks high in his classes is frequently of the 

Mental-Motive Type, which also brings forth inventors of mechanical ideas. In this group we find people who are versatile; who plan things and make them.

Yet the Mental-Motive is lacking in financial matters; thinking out practical matters and putting them into execution allow little time for consideration of return. Versatility goes with independence; and the tendency not to relp upon any subordinates. 

Education is important to the Mental-Motive Type. With out it, such people will enter fields of manual work, but will rise above the average. Many foremen and active railroad men are of the Mental-Motive type.

It must be remembered that the balance is not always even; there may be inclinations toward either the Mental or the Motive —either natural inclinations, or those brought about by environment. Thus we find Mental-Motives who seem almost Mentals; and others who seem almost Motives.

This is normal in certain cases; but with the average Mental-Motive, too little exercise will bring nervousness and worry; while neglect of thought will bring discontent. 

Naturally, the Mental-Motive person wants action and mental freedom; hence this type produces orators, political reformers, highly dramatic actors, and virile writers.

These people like ideas; they enjoy theories-provided that the theories deal with practical matters. They are wonderful in emergencies, for they think quickly and act. Where the Mental will think but do nothing; where the Motive will act but do so clumsily or wrongly, the Mental-Motive will show remarkable head-work and physical courage as well. 

Men of the Mental-Motive Type 

Men of the Mental-Motive Type are practical thinkers who divide their time between planning and executing. They have a wide range of interest; will give every subject careful consideration, and are firm and decided when they choose or act. 

They are versatile as professional men, and show an adaptability in many lines. A man of the Mental-Motive Type who has gained a good amount of knowledge will not hesitate to change his occupation to a more favorable field. His judgment is sound, and his self-confidence is strong. Among successful men of this type, the majority are those who have found their best field after they have tried several others. 44

Women of the Mental-Motive Type 

Women of this type are suited to occupational pursuits; and are capable when they try professions. If they do not enter commercial fields, they will interest themselves in social activities that have wide possibilities of development. 

They will organize clubs, or be active in such matters, always seeking to extend the range of such organizations, and to introduce physical activity. They will favor seasonable sports and entertainments, and will encourage such plans as golf in the afternoon followed by bridge games in the evening. They crave constant action, but enjoy anything that requires mental alertness.
