Depressed Curve: This is the type that curves gradually outward to the tip. It reveals an inquisitive disposition, especially if the tip is slightly raised. A downward trend of the tip shows more thoughtfulness, with a trend toward scientific study. 87

CHARACTER READING 

High Bridged, Depressed Curve: This type of Nose is very easily recognized. The bridge is high, with a noticeable bump; but immediately below the bridge, the curve is depressed and inward. This indicates an industrious nature—a certain amount of inventive power or natural resourcefulness. It is a sign of a practical mind, that works with a purpose. 

Straight Line: The Straight Line Nose may show a curve near the bridge or at the tip, but the center portion, where curves are so noticeable in the other types, is quite straight. 

This shows well-developed qualities; a courteous, respectful disposition; a natural desire to avoid trouble; and acknowledgment in the value of artistic and intellectual qualities. 

General Qualities 

Certain composite types of Noses show definite traits; and these can be summarized rather than classified. 

A combination of the High-Bridged aristocratic Nose with the Wideness of the nostrils indicates refined tastes in music and art; while the Depressed Curve with the Wideness indicates a lack of such taste.

The Slightly Raised type is an indication of the Aquiline Nose-when extremely prominent it seems beak-like and hooked at the end. It is aptly called the Commercial Nose, for the individual's desire to acquire will be most pronounced. 

This type of Nose, when thin and rigid, shows acquisitiveness to the extreme where the individual has no regard for others, and may develop a cruel, heartless nature. 

The Nose with the Bulging Tip, when straight ridged and unusually large and blunt at the end, shows willfulness overbalancing business sense; but the desire for self usually leads the individual into philosophical fields, his lack of financial judgment becoming a contempt or disregard of money matters. This is a sign of ability, which requires the management of some other person. 

The Nose that is Straight, but with long, drooping appearance denotes a melancholy disposition; while the Straight Line that has a very marked upward tilt indicates a proud disposition. 

THE MOUTH AND LIPS 

We can classify Mouths as Large, Medium, and Small. This is the first consideration; the real traits as indicated by the Mouth are those of the Lips, which are very expressive. 88

Large Mouths show an inclination toward generosity. They indicate the type of person who will listen to an appeal, and who hates to refuse such a plea.

This is a quality which is easily overcome by other traits of character; but in Character Reading, you will observe that the person with the Large Mouth will instinctively start to give toward any cause, without sensible consideration of its merits. 

Many persons of this type have overcome such tendencies; but they still feel them inwardly. 

Medium Mouths are significant of thoughtful generosity.

It requires a reasonable appeal to reach people of this type. Once convinced that they are justified in giving, they will be quite as generous as persons of the former type.

They like to contribute to worthy causes; to feel that they are putting their money or their influence in the right place. 

Small Mouths indicate a lack of generosity. There is an inclination to selfishness in this type, and when these people do give, they do so regretfully and think that a very small output is a great favor.

When they learn the advantages of generosity, they overcome this trait as far as the actual giving is concerned; but they never enjoy being generous, and will not advise it. 

Qualities of the Lips

Lips have three definite qualities: Size; Form; and Position. 

There are various sub-heads of these divisions. 

Size of the Lips

Large, Thick Lips are the sign of an ardent nature, a desirous disposition, and sensuous qualities. If the lips are rough, and puffy, it shows a tendency toward dissipation, and a nature which is difficult to control. When the lips are not over-large, and are of good appearance, great affection is portrayed. 

Medium Lips are lips of average size, quite noticeable, but not a startling feature of the Physiognomy. Such lips show a warm, affectionate nature that is controllable and moderate, yet which is capable of great desire. 

Persons with Medium Lips are choosers; they are not susceptible and do not yield to temptations; but they have desires which they do not hesitate to cultivate, and when they are in the humor they may become extremely ardent. 89

CHARACTER READING 

Small Thin Lips indicate the unconfiding, unemotional person whose idea of companionship is calm affection. Rarely, if ever, do persons of this type exhibit ardor or passion. 

Form of the Lips

Straight Lips are indicative of a well-controlled nature. They are more frequent in Thin than in Thick Lips; but whenever they occur they show a definite quality of steadiness, and the ability to curb over-desire. 

Irregular Lips show an inner nature that is troublesome, and constantly fluctuating. Persons with Lips of this type will have periods in which desire is all-possessing; and other periods of exceptional calmness and stern will. They can prepare a course and adhere firmly to it; but they are apt to yield to the unexpected.

Irregular Lips are those which are either curved, or subject to other irregularities of line, yet are similarly proportioned on each side.

Disproportioned Lips are those which show a marked difference on either side; and these may be of many variations. 

They show the lack of self-control; not only an inner conflict, but an outward one. Whether a person of this type has few desires or many, his or her tendency is to indulge constantly. 

In many cases, this is manifest in an almost entire lack of self-will; in other instances there is a conflict which is usually a losing one, and outwardly manifest in the person's actions and behavior.

Position of Lips is well observed from the Profile. The comparative positions of the Upper and Lower Lip should be carefully noted in this study. 

Protruding Upper Lip: This is the luxurious temperament; the type of person who wants wealth, whether or not he has plans for obtaining it. He is impressed by money and possession; regards them as vitally important, and shows preference for anyone who has them. 

Protruding Lower Lip: This the temperament who enjoys flattery. A person of this type values the affection of others, and considers that of greatest value. Praise is higher than wealth. 

This brings evidence also of self-esteem, and desire for selfindulgence. People with extended Lower Lips feel a certain privilege, and expect others to appreciate them as they are. 90

Even Lips: When neither the Upper nor the Lower Lip protrudes, it is a sign of discretion. Gifted with this type of Lips, the individual, while enjoying luxury, does not judge everything with wealth as a comparison.

Such persons like to be appreciated, but they regard flattery as over-enthusiasm, and discount extravagant praise.

Their dispositions, like their lips, are even. They like luxury yet will not sacrifice position to obtain it. They accept applause inwardly, realizing that it is not a great step from momentary fame to obscurity.

The Upper Lip 

Viewed from the Profile, the Upper Lip shows one of three characteristics. It is either unusually long from the nose to the lip line; or of normal distance; or extremely short from the nose to lip line.

The Long Upper Lip denotes a self-confident, self-satisfied nature; a frugal disposition, and extreme care in all matters of harboring-whether money, health, or opinion.

Persons with the long upper lip have their own opinions and are not influence by those of others.

The Medium or Average Upper Lip shows a person who has considerable endurance and who values opinions only if they are given by someone whose opinions have repeatedly shown themselves of value. A person with a Medium Upper Lip is not frugal, but wise. They do not watch pennies because they are stingy, but because they figure expenses over a long distance. 

Any indulgence, with them, must be justifiable.

The Short Upper Lip shows a person whose nature is usually good, but who is improvident and careless about the importance of the material things of life. 

They mistake flattery for opinion, and are willing to accept almost any statements that are given with an air of authority. 

They have excuses for their lack of economy in trivial matters and for their neglect of minor affairs, and they believe that an excuse is a reason. 

Notes on Lips

Protruding Lips are always a sign of strong natural appetites. This is a phsiological phenomenon. The lips of infants protrude; those of aged persons recede. 91

CHARACTER READING 

Hence with the person who is youthful or of middle age, this indication of lips is important; for it shows whether they are filled with the eager desire that characterizes early childhood; or whether they have developed the quality of satisfaction with simple necessities which is the heritage of old age. 

THE CHIN 

The Chin, as the lowest extremity of the face, has three Profile qualities which have already been observed: it is either 

Convex, Plane, or Concave.

Viewed from the side and front it has two noticeable qualities of Length or Shortness; and it also shows other features that can be easily determined by a full-face view alone. 

Length of Chin: This shows power of the passive enduring type. "Take it on the chin" is a slang expression that shows an ability to withstand hard knocks, and that is exactly what the person with the Long Chin can do. It is the Chin of the intellectual fighter.

The Short Chin: A Chin of this type lacks the stamina of withstanding difficulties. It typifies the person who may give up after a short struggle. Other qualities may give resourcefulness to the individual; but in meeting direct attack, one of this type is at a disadvantage. 

The Pointed Chin: A Pointed Chin shows quickness in perception-a certain instinctive alertness that is constantly present, whether or not it is used to great advantage. 

The Cleft Chin: This shows an ardent, selfish disposition; it typifies the person who is enthusiastic and friendly, yet who has considerable purpose in these expressions, and who thinks of personal interests. People with this type of Chin make many acquaintances, and occasionally bestow small favors, but they judge everything as of value to themselves.

The Dimpled Chin: There is energy and forcefulness in this type, but the self-will predominating. The person with the 

Dimpled Chin is friendly with mutual interest at stake, and is always alert to see that his or her personal interest is gained whether that of the other is or is not obtained. 

There is quick active affection in this type; it is easily bestowed and is ardent, but is ready to find a new affection at any time.

The Double Chin: This is the type that reveals the person who enjoys natural comforts: rest, good food and drink;

and 92

the simple, material benefits of life. It is not a luxury-craving sign; nor does it show desire for wealth.

But people who have Double Chins, or indications of them must have the foods they want-or the quantities they wantin order not to be irritable and disagreeable. 

References Regarding Chins Some authorities on Character Analysis include four other types with those described above. They will be summarized here, but two of them represent opposite phases of Profile; and two represent types of Formation. 

The Receding Chin (Convex Profile): Impatience; hurried action; sudden ideas; changeability.

The Projecting Chin (Concave Profile): Deliberate activity; 

patience; slowness; obstinacy.

The Broad Chin (Square Form): Dependability in thought and action; tenacity; indomitable spirit, and determination. 

The Narrow Chin (Triangular Form): Thought; mental determination; mental activity.

Summary 

In considering types of Chins, include the last four, remembering that they show definite characteristics when the Chin is studied as a separate subject. 

But the qualities of such Chins are usually already understood from analyses of Formation and Profile. 

The distinctive types of Chins: Long, Short, Pointed, Cleft, Dimples, and Double, show qualities which belong distinctly to Chin Analysis. 

THE EARS 

Of all the features, the ears are least important from the standpoint of expression, for they have virtually nothing to do with the face itself.

Yet criminologists have learned that the ears form a veritable index to moral and intellectual character of the individual. The ears are not mobile, subject to change and form of expression, like the other features. Even the Chin-the least expressive of the facial features-is slightly subject to muscular alteration. 

But the Analysis of the Ears has a peculiar significance, because here we are dealing with a phase of Physiognomy that does not alter. The Ears-like the spots of the leopard-cannot change. 93

CHARACTER READING 

It must be remembered, however, that the character of man is something that develops. Each individual is virtually a product of his own evolution. His ears may give no more an idea of his present nature than the fossilized skeleton of a cave-man would tell us of the human race of today. 

The shape of his ears may be a product of heredity, indicative of a hidden trait that has passed through several generations. 

We cannot deny that the trait is there; whether it is active or not is another question.

This is particularly true because Ear Analysis deals with the more primitive traits, and shows underlying characteristics. 

The reason that criminologists have studied Ear Shapes is because so many of their subjects are admittedly of inferior mental development. We can, therefore, make this rule: 

The importance of Character Reading by Ear Analysis de creases in proportion to the development of intelligence in the subject.

More than thirty distinct types may be noted in a study of the Ears. The lobes, particularly, show variation. The principal differences are grouped here in about a dozen easily recognized classes.

Size of the Ear 

It is easy to tell whether the Ears are Large, Medium or Small. The average Ear can be called Medium. Large Ears and Small Ears are quite evident.

The Large Ear is a sign of preference for intellectual pursuits. It shows a mind that rises above animal nature, and indicates a person who is primarily a thinker, even though his pursuits of intellect may vary from veneration and the religious nature to the shrewd plans of a schemer. 

The Medium Ear is significant of broadness in views. It represents the person who has a variety of interests, and who is about evenly interested in the thoughtful side of life and the physical activities of primitive existence. 

The Small Ear shows a crude aggressive instinct, and typi fies the individual who lives on the physical plane. Even in persons of intelligent types, the primitive viewpoint is evident, if they have Small Ears.

The Long Ear, which is usually of the Large type shows a theoretical nature, which is apt to deal in impractical ideas. This is seldom seen in the Small type; but frequently in the Medium. 94

The Wide Ear, generally of the Small type, sometimes Medium, but rarely Large indicates an extremely practical and matter-of-face disposition.

The Pointed Ear which comes to a peak at the top, is a very reliable sign of shrewdness and cunning. It is fox-like, and the similarity is a true one. Dishonesty is a quality that is often discovered in persons with this type of Ear. With the Small 

Ear, such a trait is easily distinguished; but the Ear is usually 

Long and either Medium or Large. If it is Long, Large, and 

Pointed-then one may be sure that the individual is capable of continued scheming, and that his or her mind is frequently planning such methods with an urge to put them into practice. 

The formation of the upper portion of the Ear shows mental activities; hence in the Pointed Ear type the individual is sure to be shrewd and cunning, with alertness of mind.

Close Ears are those which lie close to the head, and can be quickly detected from the back. They show a cautious, conservative and provident disposition. 

Spread Ears that stand out from the head show a penchant for originality which is dependent upon the character of the individual. The person of high intellect whose Ears are of this type will be responsible for many ingenious plans and ideas on life. 

With individuals of primitive traits, this tendency for originality will be seen in dress and attire. 

The Lobes of the Ears are either Complete; Partial; or Undeveloped. These are differences that are very noticeable. 

The Complete Lobe (sometimes called the Large Lobe) shows a responsive disposition. People with such Lobes grasp ideas, and are strong and self-reliant. In this type of Ear the 

Lobe is virtually an appendage that hangs from the Ear itself. 

The Partial Lobe is quite noticeable but it is not large, and appendage-like. It is partly connected with the head, yet is definitely a Lobe.

It reveals the qualities of cleverness and artistic thought, but shows an opinionated or narrow nature. Response is present to a degree, and there is an aggressiveness in a person who has the 

Partial Lobe. 

The Undeveloped Lobe is virtually no Lobe at all. The ear itself seems to meet the head just at the spot where the Lobe should begin.

This shows lack of development-a nature which is subject to many difficulties if high ideals are lacking. It indicates a 95

CHARACTER READING 

tendency to crude behavior, and shows lack of responsiveness. 

Many persons of incorrigible nature and low habits have this type of Lobe.

The Overdeveloped Lobe, a huge appendage much too large in proportion to the Ear is a very poor indication, as it is the type generally seen in savage races. 

THE TEETH 

There is a distinct relationship between the Teeth and Physiognomy; but the Teeth themselves can hardly be studied in 

Character Reading. It will interest the reader to know that students of dentistry have classified the formation of the head and face in terms resembling the types of Triangular, Square and Circular, showing that these groupings are scientific. 

From the teeth we can learn head shapes; and from head shapes, the forms of teeth may be learned. Hence the Character 

Analyst can gain nothing further by studying the shapes of the teeth, as they would serve chiefly as a check-up on Formation. 

The condition of the teeth varies a great deal with individuals, and cannot serve as an accurate guide except by long study and close examination.

In relation to the mouth, however, it is noted that there are instances in which the lips are rarely closed, and here we have two distinct types: Partly Opened Lips, which give a glimpse of the upper teeth; and Fully Opened Lips, which show both lower and upper teeth.

When Upper Teeth are visible, it is a sign of a cold, undemonstrative nature-indicating a person who is not enthusiastic and who either lacks or is indifferent to the art of gaining warm, affectionate friends.

When Upper and Lower Teeth are visible, we have the eccentric, radical and sometimes dangerous type of person who is likely to do the unexpected at the most surprising time.

FACIAL EXPRESSION 

In concluding our study of Physiognomy, we are naturally impressed with the composite formation of the human character. 

So many keys and guides to traits and foibles are present that the rapid reader may think the subject is confusing. 

Such is not the case. The fact that a great quantity of material is available is a wonderful thing. The Character Analyst is the 96

owner of a tremendous store of knowledge, and he has the opportunity of using it in part or in its entirety. 

In studying a subject, look for salient features. A nose of a distinctive type carries an important message; an ear may tell a graphic story.

The systematic Character Reader runs rapidly through the methods of analysis in about the order they have been given here. 

If he sees a man of the distinct Motive Type, he has a real analysis to begin with. But if the type is composite-particularly Mental-Motive-Vital, he does not enter into an intricate study of the combined Type; instead he immediately looks for salient points in 

Profile; failing to find them, he is sure to learn much through Physiognomy.

Others apply Character Reading in a less definite way. They are not desirous of making a complete analysis; they merely wish to know some important facts concerning the traits of the individual with whom they are dealing.

They immediately recognize some factor either in Formation, Profile, or Physiognomy and use it to practical advantage. 

The first lessons are also instructive; the differences in Masculine and Feminine; in Blondes and Brunettes. But these methods while interesting, will be dealt with later on. The final consideration of this section is that of Facial Expression. 

The Active Face is one that is highly expressive. It shows a nervous type; the individual whose traits and characteristics are on the surface. With such an individual, you may be sure that anything you discover in analyzing will soon make itself evident. 

Such a person's character is outward, revealing itself with no thought of concealment. You can learn to know such a person; to understand such a person; and you will find that people who are not versed in Character Reading will also have some very definite and not far wrong-impressions of that individual's tendencies.

The Passive Face is another matter; and here the observation of the Character Reader is of greatest avail. The Passive Face is popularly termed the "Poker Face," because the individual who possesses it has a wonderful ability for concealing his thoughts. 

Here the traits and characteristics of the individual are deepset, hidden below the surface. These people are difficult for the average person to understand, and they are often disliked by persons who should admire them, and admired by persons who would dislike them if they knew their true characters. 97

CHARACTER READING To the Character Reader, the Passive Face presents a wonder ful study. It is a calm face that generally preserves its normal appearance, and the passivity which is vexing to the untrained observer is an aid to the Character Analyst.

By studying those inflexible, placid features, he can soon solve the riddle of the individual's tendencies. If certain evidences of 

Formation, Profile, or Physiognomy are evident, the Analyst will soon have them classified; and will realize that he knows the inward thoughts of an individual whom most persons regard as inscrutable.

People with Passive Faces are often the best to have as friends, because theid composure shows deep-set character. There are others of this type who are extremely dangerous; and to understand them is of tremendous value.

Hence Character Reading is both enlightening and protective —a scientific study which becomes an artistic accomplishment in the person who applies it; a knowledge that gives him an insight on life and enables him to understand many things of value and importance.

PHRENOLOGY 

Phrenology is a system of brain study discovered in 1796 by Franz Joseph Gall, a physician of Vienna, who, after examining the formation of many heads, worked out correlations between the topography of the skull and traits of character. 

The primal theory of Phrenology, as developed by Gall and his followers, is that the brain is a multiplex structure, with fortytwo constituent centers. A prominence in the skull above any such center indicates a high development of the faculty beneath. 

The difficulty of applying a knowledge of Phrenology lies in the fact that the centers as defined by Gall are quite small, and a minute examination of the head is necessary. Peculiar formations of the skull may account for certain protuberances, and make correct interpretation difficult.

But these are problems encountered in the study of Phrenology itself. The Character Reader, who depends upon observation, does not have to resort to close inspection of the cranium. Phrenology is of interest to him purely because Gall built up his thories and rules by experimental research and study in head formation, and thereby proved that noticeable proportions of the head have definite significance. 98

We have already made a partial analysis of Head Shapes. The present method is to work backward from the intricate classifications of Phrenology and thus group them into major Head Divisions.

REGION OF THE MORAL OR SPIRITUAL SENTIMENTS REGION OF THE REGION OF THE PROPENSITIES INTELLECTUAL FACULTIES The Three Major Divisions in the Organs of the Brain. 

The first chart shows the forty-two centers of Phrenology, which is of use to the student of Character Analysis, because he can draw a reasonable conclusion from any large proportion. 

But the second chart is of a more general nature, grouping the various centers into the three Major Divisions of Propensities; 

Sentiments; and Intellectual Faculties. 99

CHARACTER READING The Propensities 

These are the passions, and animal instincts. This Division is located at the bottom of the skull, particularly in the posterior region.

The Sentiments 

The Sentiments, or Emotions, form a Major Division at the top of the head. These are the qualities of a high nature, governed partly by reason, but chiefly by enlightenment or natural emotion of spiritual type in opposition to animal desire. 

The Intellectual Faculties 

The Intellectual Faculties occupy the center portion of the head, between the Sentiments and Propensities. The Active Faculties are located at the front of the head; the Passive Faculties are at the back.

100

Types of Division Qualities 

The chart with the forty-two divisions serves as a guide to the component parts of the Major Head Divisions. We may summarize the three regions thus:

Propensities 

Active motion, and love of food forward; passion and sexuality low and in back; parental love in back, bordering on the 

Division of Intellectual Faculties. Courage is a quality partly in the Region of Propensities, just in back of the ear. 

The Sentiments 

These include human nature, benevolence, dignity, justice, and high qualities of imitation, with a partial presence of homeloving and social qualities.

The Intellectual Faculties 

The powers of reason are at the front of the head, high on the forehead; below, in the center of the forehead, are the qualities of memory and retention; with quick perception beneath. 

Imaginative ability shows itself in the front half of the center section; caution and wise courage are in the rear half of the center section, while friendship and love of home life are at the posterior of this Division. 

Comparison of Major Divisions 

The average, or Ideal Head, should show an equal, balanced development of the three Major Divisions. Proportion is the key to this study. A large head does not show excessive mental development in any of the qualities; nor does a small head indicate deficiency.

It is the proportionate relationship of one division to the others that must be considered, in judging whether a Major Division may be classified as Large or Small. 

Large Division of Propensities 

Such a Division shows a desire for excess in eating and drinking. It indicates a powerful, emphatic nature, which, when aroused will become destructive and dangerous. 

People of this type are defiant and combative. They are usually of good physique and rely on force to carry their desires. 

With them, might is right. 101

CHARACTER READING 

They are ardent in love, and show great animosity toward any rival. They become parents who will show great love for their children but who will take the part of a child even when it is wrong or in conflict with other children. There is no impartiality in this type of person. Such people stand up for their own interests, all the time.

While persons of this type have native courage, they are afraid of death and the unknown. They are apt to be superstitious to an extreme.

Small Division of Propensities 

A small Division on Propensities reveals the person who is lacking in simple courage, who has little mechanical inclination, and who eat and drink comparatively little. 

They are not fond of children-even their own-and are usually unsympathetic toward them. They are apt to be inefficient, and inactive, and often unable to defend themselves against overbearing persons.

Large Division of Sentiments 

In this type, we find persons who love truth; who have high ethical standards, and who are optimistic.

They have a great regard for ideals, and are strong in their religious inclinations, believing that such fundamentals are of the greatest value in life.

Being also imitative, they generally abide by the tenets of some religious denomination; are sincere and strong in their ideas, and are not easily swayed to doubt.

They are conscientious and try to live up to their beliefs. 

They feel that life is good, and that the hereafter will bring reward. They try to take a personal part in community life, and seek to promote harmony and benevolence.

They seek to put ideals in everything, including business, and will often let their interests suffer because their consciences so guide them. They seek dignity in all undertakings and will uphold something that they believe is right no matter how impractical it may be.

They are over-idealistic, and are very easily shocked by matters of small consequence. They admire persons of religious tendencies and cannot distinguish between the sincere person and the hypocrite. 102

Small Division of Sentiments 

Persons of this type lack conscientious scruples. They are doubters and are interested neither in religion nor self-sacrifice. 

They are neither sympathetic nor respectful, and they regard truthfulness as a useless scruple. They are not untrustworthy in all their actions, but they are willing to sacrifice truth, honor and dignity before anything else, for intangible ideals are to them of little value.

They seek favorable judgments that will help them, and do not care whether or not the decisions are fair. They are selfseeking, and therefore have very little sense of gratitude. 

When they set certain standards of morality or behavior they will live up to them, purely because they have developed in their own minds. Hence their ideals, while generally differing from accepted standards, are usually well defined-but patterned for their own convenience.

Large Division of Intellectual Faculties 

Large development of the central Major Division shows a critical, reasoning, individual ability. It typifies the searcher who wishes to examine and inspect.

Persons of this type have the practical Head Development; 

they can perceive and calculate, and they can remember. 

Their minds are orderly, keeping small facts in arrangement and analyzing events or happenings. People with this Large 

Head Division are not content with superficial knowledge. 

The mind predominates over animal nature, and guides all actions. People of this type seek experience, yet appreciate home. Their courage is of a mental type, and they have cautious instincts.

Yet they pay so much attention to details that they are apt to worry over trifling matters, and to waste their efforts in minute affairs. They are clever in their methods and are argumentative. 

Small Division of Intellectual Faculties 

Persons of this type overlook little things that explain greater ones. They are not perceptive and they do not reason logically. When learning a subject they ask many questions and fail to understand readily.

They cannot see points of similarity nor can they draw fine distinctions. They lack caution and will proceed blindly into dangers. They are heedless of advice. 103

CHARACTER READING 

They lack social instincts, and have a very poorly developed sense of humor. They do not plan well, and they are easily bewildered. They are forgetful and generally do not make any effort to remember things.

Average Major Divisions 

Average Divisions of the head show a normal amount of tendency in the particular quality represented. Thus a person who approaches the balance in all three will possess the qualities of those who have Large Divisions, but will have such qualities in a moderate degree. Largeness is a sign of quality presence; Smallness indicates lack of qualities.

In studying the head, remember that because one Major Division is large, the others are not necessarily small. Similarly, the fact that one Major Division is large and another is actually small may make one appear to be overly large and the other average. 

The proper method is to estimate by observation just how the head should be proportioned, and then decide upon the development or lack of development of the sections. When doubtful, it is best to regard the section as Average, for Large or Small tendencies are usually quite noticeable.

Summary Head Divisions explain much to the Character Analyst. He will often meet two persons who have similar ideas but different opinions on them: for example, Mr. A may show a definite indifference to certain ethical standards, admitting that he considers them worthless, but showing no desire to have them altered. Mr. 

B, on the contrary, may denounce such accepted standards, and indignantly demand that others be set up instead. 

In analyzing these two individuals, Mr. A will show a Small 

Division of Sentiments. Mr. B, however, will have a Large Division of Intellectual Faculties. A is not ethical because he has no sympathy with such standards; B reasons that the particular standard under discussion is not logical. 

Similar contrasts in individuals are seen in many other ways; and the student of Head Divisions will find explanations for many characteristics in persons.

The Character Reader is not a Phrenologist. He is actually a person who understands the basic principles of Head Divisions and uses them in addition to Physiognomy, Profile, etc. in studying the characteristics of persons by observation. 104

The Phrenologist may know nothing about the varied forms of Character Analysis; his whole thought is centered on reducing the three Major Head Divisions to their component parts and gaining conclusions by minute analysis. 

Hence the pros and cons of Phrenology do not enter into Character Reading. Gall, in his investigations, began with the 

Major Head Divisions, and their obvious importance and definite significance led him further in his theories and studies. The Character Analyst takes those same principal groups and applies them to his other observations. Hence Character Reading and Phrenology, as distinctive branches of study, may be said to overlap, or to meet at their extremes. 

CHIROGNOMY 

Chirognomy is the study of the Hand and its indications, just as Physiognomy is the study of the face. Much can be learned from Chirognomy, and it serves as a valuable method of comparison in the study of an individual whose Head and Face have been analyzed.

Chirognomy is quite distinct from Palmistry, which is a study of the lines of the hands, and the mounts. The student of 

Palmistry uses the basic principles of Chirognomy in his first survey of the hand; but the Character Reader employs Chirognomy alone.

Even without the knowledge gained from Physiognomy, a keen observer could gain real results in the study of character by considering the hand alone.

The Normal Hand 

As a basis for Character Reading by the Hand, we have the Normal Hand, in which the fingers are straight and well-formed, and the thumb likewise. The palm is slightly wider than the fingers when pressed together, and they are about the same length as the palm from the heel to the base of the fingers. 

From this Normal, or Basic Hands, we can judge three types of Hands which show the Triangular, Square, and Circular Formation indicating Mental, Motive and Vital Types.

Triangular Form: Mental Type 

Such hands are long, slender, tapering and sometimes delicate; the fingers are of the Conic or Pointed form. Such hands denote the thinker, who is interested in mental pursuits. 105

CHARACTER READING Square Form: Motive Type 

This is the hand with heavy set, square proportions. The fingers are Square—not Pointed, but ending abruptly; or it may be Spatulate, with enlarged tips. The hand is usually bony and rough.

Such hands show the purposeful, punctual individual who is industrious and active; capable of manual effort. In the Spatulate form, which is really an exaggeration of the Square form, high activity is present.

Circular Form: Vital Type 

This hand is very close to the Normal Hand; the fingers are not long, and they do not show marked Square tendencies; sometimes they incline slightly to the Pointed type. But the whole hand is full and fleshy.

This indicates the Vital Type-the person who is commercially inclined; easy going, yet fond of good living and comfortable surroundings.

The Psychic Hand is very long, and pointed; it represents the extreme of the Triangular Form, and shows the dreamy, idealistic person, to whom all toil is effort, and who is apt to be visionary and impractical.

The Conic Hand, less extreme than the Psychic Hand, shows an artistic, generous and somewhat temperamental nature. It is a type resembling the Triangular Form, but shows enthusiastic, changeable inclinations, with emotional attributes. 

The Philosophic Hand is large, knotted and midway between the Square and Conic (which is markedly Triangular). It shows the studious, independent thinker, who is not enthusiastic or emotional, but who reasons logically and thoroughly. 

The Elemental Hand is short, blunt and clumsy, with thick, stiff fingers and a club-shaped thumb. It reveals a primitive type of person, inclined to be crude and violent. 

The Mixed Hand shows varieties of different types-and it indicates mingled characteristics. Unlike the Normal Hand, which is seldom seen, and represents the Balance, the Mixed Hand is composite. It is typical of the Mental-Motive-Vital, or two of those types.

The Fingers show traits of the individual, and may be studied in detail; but a summary of their most important qualities will prove of more value than long discussions. 106

Knotty Joints indicate the philosophic tendency; love of order, neatness and deliberation.

Persons with such fingers analyze carefully, with real desire to arrive at facts. They are not impulsive, nor inspired. They have scientific tendencies, and are seldom romantic. 

Smooth Fingers lack bulging joints; that is their status of classification. They indicate intuition, and artistic tastes, but show a lack of analytical reasoning. Persons with this type of fingers make pleasant companions, and are spontaneous in their desires. They are quick and responsive.

Long Fingers show perception, interest in details, and powers of memory. Persons with such fingers are usually graceful, or possessed of digital dexterity, and capable of fine artistic work with the hands. They are painstaking and patient. 

Short Fingers show a quickness and desire for results. They are a sign of impulsiveness and a tendency to jump to quick conclusions. These people are lively, and avoid detail work. They are brief in speech and definite in their remarks. 

The Thumb should come under careful observation, for it tells many traits of the individual. Its first indication is judged by Highness or Lowness of the point where it extends from the hand.

The High Set Thumb shows a low order of intelligence. It shows lack of ability, and poorness of mental qualities. 

The Low Set Thumb indicates an intense nature, filled with human sympathy and understanding; with high grade of mental quality. 

The Medium Set Thumb indicates frankness, capable mentality, boldness and independence. It shows the person who is reasonably responsive, and who has well-balanced views. 

The Long Thumb shows a generous disposition and a development of existing characteristics.

The Short Thumb shows a close, selfish nature that is slow in its development. 

The Wide Set Thumb that hangs well away from the hand reveals the talkative, expressive person who makes friends quickly and who is quite gracious.

The Close Set Thumb which seems to adhere to the side of the hand, represents a person who is hard to approach, who is cautious, secretive and often narrow in views. 107

CHARACTER READING 

The Well Formed Thumb is nicely shaped and is immediately recognized by its attractiveness. It shows a person of pleasing disposition, who does not like to wound the feelings of others, and who is considerate. It is one of the best signs of good character. 

The Elemental Thumb, clumsy, ugly and ill-shapen is a sign of brute force in both action and reason. It indicates obstinacy, lack of fairness, and tactless, cumbersome methods. It is a sign of doubtful character and should warn one against the individual. 

The Strong Thumb is not one of fine, well-modeled shape; but neither is it ugly. It is rather large and heavy, and shows an aggressive mentality. It is a sign of determination, and although it may indicate obstinacy, it shows fairness and reason in addition to strong will.

General Characteristics of the Hand 

The General Characteristics of the Hand apply to the fingers and thumb in modified degree. The main impression that one gains of the Hand in action is its flexibility or stiffness.

The Flexible Hand is one of great pliability; it bends backward at the fingers, and the joints are very loose. 

The Flexible Hand shows a person of versatility, who is capable of accomplishment. It indicates an adaptive personality. 

Persons with Flexible Hands are not dreamers, but they are exaggerators, and are fond of fanciful stories.

They are impressionistic and grasp ideas rapidly. Yet they are apt to scatter their interests and to show a lack of definite procedure. They are usually generous, often spend money foolishly, and are intrigued by sudden desire. They are rapid workers, and are changeable in their views. Unusual flexibility of the uppermost joints show special cleverness and quickness in intellectual pursuits.

The Extreme Flexible Hand which is double jointed shows a poetic or theoretical nature; a talent for music or for other arts, and a love of variety.

It is symbolic of a fickle nature, and usually indicates lack of judgment in matters concerning money. Persons with such hands are often lost in their ideas and are difficult to understand. 

The Straight Hand opens to its full extent so that it forms a straight line; it may go a trifle backward, but not without noticeable effort. 108

This is a sign of self-containment; it indicates good judgment and an even disposition, not inclined to be enthusiastic, but full of genuine interest.

There is nothing extreme in persons who have this type of hand. They are progressive, capable, and suited to professional work. They are willing to spend money, but do not hesitate to get what is due them. They listen to what is told them and understand it for what it is worth.

The Stiff Hand does not open beyond a slight curve. It has inward tendencies, and shows a firm, conservative nature. 

Persons with this type of hand are capable of long, heavy, plodding effort; they gain definite ideas and keep them; but they are apt to move in a narrow field, cramping themselves and being satisfied with it.

They are economical, and often stingy. They believe that success is attained through economy; and they are not talkative. 

They settle with definite habits and stick to them; and they are very difficult to change.

They cannot be called selfish, but as a rule they are ungenerous, and save up everything they earn, even if they have to deprive themselves of comforts. They like to work for big concerns where slow advancements and pensions lie ahead. 

They are satisfied with old ideas and customs; they are usually lacking in artistic accomplishments; but they are active and progressive along their definite line of endeavor. 

Color and Texture 

The Color and Texture of Hands bear out the distinctions given in the section on General Characteristics. The hands may be used as a basis for judgment as well as the face. 

Hairy hands show Coarseness, and Masculine Qualities; while smooth, hairless hands indicate Fineness and Feminine Qualities. 

The same is true of the Finger Nails: if rough, clumsy and brittle, they indicate Coarseness; if smooth, finely shaped, and soft, they indicate Fineness.

In judging for Texture in particular, bear in mind that 

Coarseness-being Masculine-will be much more evident in men than in women. A man may have hairy hand, and still show 

Fineness in Texture. A woman's skin may be smooth and hairless, yet be obviously Coarse, when compared with the Fine qualities so frequently evident in women. 109

CHARACTER READING POSTURE AND GESTURE 

Posture is a person's carriage and bearing; Gesture is his active conduct. Hence Posture and Gesture are physical expressions of existing Character.

Hitherto we have dealt with Character Reading from the study of existing features that are indelibly on record on the individual. We have touched a bit on development when we considered Feature Line of the face, and Expression of the Features. 

But in Posture and Gesture, particularly the latter, we find the tendencies of the individual making themselves manifest. They prove what we can learn beforehand through the basic methods of Character Analysis. 

Human beings are usually in one of two humors. They are either Active or Passive. By this we do not mean that they are either working or resting; but that in their activities they assume one of the two methods, which can be readily distinguished. 

Some individuals are distinctly of the Active Type; others are of the Passive Type. To understand the significance of these differences, both must be defined. 

Active Posture and Gesture

In Active Posture and Gesture, a person seeks expansion. He spreads his body, and extends his arms. He seems to be reaching out or upward, and hence the various parts of his body are free and in motion.

This indicates the person of desire; the man or woman of inspiration, who is capable of sudden motion and quick progress. 

It is typical of freedom of thought; argument, and conviction. 

But it also betrays wasted effort, intense emotion, and eccentricity. 

People who have this form of Posture and Gesture are enthusiastic and excitable, but easily swayed. The same power that leads them to convince others makes them seize upon new theories without careful consideration. They make quick decisions and go to work rapidly, but are often ineffectual except when they have gained a sudden inspiration that is workable.

Passive Posture and Gesture 

The person of the opposite type is thoughtful, meditative and careful. His tendency is to draw in his arms, bend his head, and hunch his body. 110

Such people have ability of concentration, and are practical minded-not inspired. They are deliberate and unemotional. 

Their fault is restriction, and narrowness. They are apt to be moody and despondent. They will let opportunities pass by because their ideas are confined to cramped interests. 

Typical Examples 

The Active Type is indicated by the man who throws his shoulders back; who spreads his arms over the side of a chair, who extends his feet, and who keeps his head turned up. He is usually open-minded, but ready to present objections to statements. 

The Passive Type is indicated by the man who stoops, folds his arms; who draws his feet beneath a chair; and who keeps his head bent down. This signifies the closed mind; the man who is apparently listening and not objecting, but who is secretly paying little attention to anything that is told him. 

General Types 

The Reliant Type is the man or woman who has upright carriage, who is normally active but never eccentric. Such people carry their arms rather stiffly at their sides, yet have a natural ease of motion and a firmness and definiteness in Gesture. They can be depended upon, and are efficient and methodical. 

The Dignified Type is extreme in upright carriage-unusually firm in Posture, yet with Passive tendencies, such as a slight inclination of the head.

Such people carry their arms stiffly, have very little ease of motion, and are affected in Gesture. They are not so efficient or methodical as the Reliant Type, and are apt to be conceited and too self-satisfied. They are only dependable when their own interests or importance is at stake. 

The Undecided Type is recognized by wavering Posture and unbalanced Gesture. Such persons are of an Active Type, but they are not methodical in their motions. One arm is usually slightly raised while the other stays close to the body. They present study in uncertainty. They do not like to make quick decisions or to meet an issue.

The Secretive Type is Passive. Individuals of this sort keep their hands behind them or out of sight, and have a way of looking upward when the head is bowed.

They are suspicious and watchful, always suspecting something that will injure their interests. When such Posture and 111

CHARACTER READING 

Gesture are particularly evident, it is a sign of a deceitful, untrustworthy person.

The Determined Type is normally Active in Posture and extremely definite in Gesture. The hands are usually slightly clenched. Carriage is erect; motions are energetic. These people are purposeful and insist on obtaining what they desire. 

The Artistic Type is indicated by gracefulness of motion. 

People of this class are Active in Posture and Gesture and assume unaffected poses without effort.

They are clever, intelligent, but emotional and temperamental; and they are inclined to exaggeration or sudden enthusiasm. 

The Nervous Type has a Passive tendency in Posture, with Active Gesture. The contrast is immediately noticed. The body is often stooped, and the head bent; but the hands are shifting continually and seem to be worrying what to do. 

Such persons are very easily excited. They are afraid of a crisis, and worry about issues before they arrive. They are restless and have extreme dislikes for certain things. 

The Pugnacious Type is an extreme of the Determined Type. 

The posture is Active, and Gesture is sudden and rapid. Persons of this type are constantly seeing opposition and are always ready to start trouble.

The Calm Type is the opposite of the Nervous Type; Active in Posture but Passive in Gesture. Such persons have good carriage, keep their heads up, and survey everything steadily; but they show very little motion.

They are patient and never restless; they do not worry, and they are equal to emergencies.

The Affected Type is artificially active. Posture is assumed; every Gesture seems timed and planned; in fact the whole bearing apes the Artistic Type. 

Persons of this class are insincere and fond of pretence. They cannot be relied upon in important matters, and they often hide their true feelings. When observed in men, this is a sign of shallowness and superficiality.

The Hands in Gesture As the hands may be called the instruments of Gesture, they frequently betray a person's innermost feelings. The hands are used to express ideas such as beckoning and making other signs. 

The hand is truly the servant of the mind; and the study of its 112

action is a branch of Chirognomy as well as a part of Posture and 

Gesture. 

Partly clenched fists, we have observed, show determination; while those that are tightly closed show pugnacity. As the opposite extreme, hands which are limp, with dangling fingers, show the person who is lazy-both physically and mentally-and who avoids conflict.

Hands with tightly closed fingers portray a secretive nature. 

Hands that are limp, particularly with the fingers wide apart, show instability-they betray the person who tells everything he hears.

Hands that have the fingers gently closed, and are not overlimp, show a person who is trustworthy and discreet when the occasion demands it.

Hands behind the back denote caution; hands hidden indicate self-consciousness and deceit; hands that.rub together frequently are a sign of hypocricy.

Calm hands often rest together; active hands are moving a great deal of the time; and nervous hands are moving everywhere. 

SUMMARY 

10pt. caslon italic-center 

Through Posture and Gesture and observations of the motions of the hands, you can gain many useful inferences in Reading Character. The hands are particularly good as a guide, because they can often be watched when the person is otherwise inactive. 

All people are not extremes of Types; therefore one cannot make satisfactory observations under all conditions. When a person is thinking, or actively engaged in something of interest, Posture and Gesture will reveal themselves; and other tendencies can be marked during periods of repose.

Observations in Walking 

Certain traits are revealed by a person's walk, which is identified with the carriage of Posture.

The fast walker, who has swinging, moving arms, and a rhythm of gait, is energetic and determined. 

Short, quick steps indicate impatience and nervous energy of the individual.

Slow, long steps with a motionless body denote a thoughtful, meditative nature, and a penchant for carefulness. 

A loose-jointed, slovenly walk shows an unimaginative mind and a careless, lazy disposition. 113

CHARACTER READING 

Slow, heavy walking, that is not graggy, but seems weighty, shows a person who is intellectually slow and stubborn. 

A shambling person who walks irregularly and unevenly lacks decision and concentrated effort.

Observations in Laughter 

Different people have different ways of laughing. Everyone realizes this; but few attempt to classify persons by their laughs. 

Yet this is an excellent way to obtain a quick impression of an individual.

A good-natured, sincere sort of a person has a deep laugh which is not loud or boisterous, but whole-hearted. This is a sign of a good, friendly disposition.

Persons who are calm and self-possessed laugh mildly and easily, but do not long remain mirthful. 

The giggler is a self-conscious, frivolous person who is easily embarrassed and who is worried over trifles.

Those who laugh loudly, and never smile, are usually of coarse, primitive natures.

A short, gasping sort of laugh is an expression of a seriousminded person.

A high, hysterical laugh indicates a nervous temperament and a person inclined to be selfish.

Those who smile but do not laugh are of cynical and critical disposition; while others, who laugh at everything, are mentally shallow and unstable.

The person who laughs long but not often, and who has periods of silent laughter with recurrence, is of a kind, sympathetic, sincere nature.

GRAPHOLOGY Graphology, or Character Reading Revealed by Handwriting is a long, specialized subject in itself. It is useful in Character 

Analysis only when one does not have an opportunity to see the individual who is the subject of analysis. 

Handwriting experts have shown amazing ability to interpret the characteristics of individuals, and the subject of Graphology deals with numerous fine distinctions that enable one versed in this science to gain an almost perfect impression of the writer. 114

The Character Reader should therefore be conversant with the most important incitations of the most common forms of handwriting. These will prove sufficient for practical reference. 

Angular Handwriting is recognized by its pointed letters and sharp, straight lines.

It indicates a quick, energetic disposition. People who write in this style are practical and enthusiastic as a rule, and are usually industrious. These traits are subject to modification by other features of writing. 

Rounded Handwriting, composed almost entirely of curves, shows a fondness for beauty. It typifies the person who enjoys ease, who is peaceful and who likes comfort. These traits are subject to modification by other features of writing. 

Backward Handwriting in which the letters lean to the left shows a reasoning mind; a person who is not particularly affectionate, and who may be a schemer. It shows a lack of enthusiasm. 

1. Note the upward slant. 

2. Well rounded and level. 

3. Small writing leaning backward. 

4. Large but poorly spaced. 

5. This is harty and downward. 

6. Note how this writing is crowded.

Upright Writing shows a deliberate, self-restrained person who is not emotional, and who is apt to be slow and careful in action. It shows a conscientious nature-that of a person who is not shaken or disturbed in outlook on life. 115

CHARACTER READING 

Forward Writing is the commonest type, and does not portray any marked characteristics, unless the slant is very noticeable. 

That indicates a person of the emotional type, who is easily pleased and highly enthusiastic. 

Heavy Handwriting in which pressure has obviously been used on the pen stands for self-assurance, and indicates the person who is aggressive. While this last characteristic may be a good one in the individual, it may also show domineering and overbearing tendencies. The person with the heavy handwriting usually has determination.

Large Handwriting indicates the person who has big ideas; whether he is capable of executing them is another matter. It shows critical, fault-finding traits, and a tactless disposition. A person who writes in this manner is usually frank and outspoken. 

Small Handwriting means originality. Those who write in such a manner are usually good reasoners, with ideas of culture and intellectuality. Largeness in writing is common with the uneducated; hence smallness goes with refinement and studiousness. 

It is a sign of diplomacy and patience.

Uniform Writing in which the letters are carefully formed and very neatly done, shows the precise mind. It signifies a methodical person, who is economical, and of good commercial ability. 

Uneven Writing in which no great attention is paid to letter formation shows carelessness, especially in small matters. 

This writing is not illegible; it simply varies, and shows clearly that the writer is not precise nor exacting; and that he is apt to be indifferent toward many matters. 

Hurried Writing is often illegible. It shows the person whose mind works rapidly, who is a good conversationalist, and whose active mind runs ahead of his hand when he uses the pen. 

It is not a sign of carelessness, but merely the desire to hurry through details. Such a writer cannot stand the annoyance of slow expression, and tries to keep his writing up with his thoughts.

Crowded Writing is a distinct indication of the thrifty mind. 

A person who dislikes waste instinctively pushes words together, especially at the right hand side of the page. The lines are often close together.

This shows a rather secretive disposition, and often indicates a suspicious nature. 116

Spread Writing that runs very few lines to a page shows persons who are naturally extravagant, and often generous. They are generally unsystematic and lacking in method; they dislike painstaking effort and are fond of social life. 

Upward Writing that climbs above regular lines shows a cheery sort of person, who is ambitious and has ideals. Such a writer is always looking for accomplishment, and has faith in his ability to get it. 

Level Handwriting indicates some degree of firmness and perseverence on the part of the writer. It shows the person who unconsciously follows a regular line of thought and keeps up to it, centering his attention on the occupation at hand. 

Downward Handwriting tells of an easily discouraged person.

It indicates a tendency toward pessimism, and gives an inkling of fits of depression. Such a person does not have an over-abundance of self-reliance. 

Wide Margins show the individual of artistic temperament, especially if the margins are regular all the way down the pagein which case neatness and tidiness are indicated.

Joined Letters in which the letters of a word are all connected signify a skeptical person who has definite views on life and who is exacting, being an individual of good judgment. This quality if writing is accentuated if there is a tendency to join words. It shows that the pen stays close to the paper. 

Separated Letters in the midst of words shows an inspirational nature. They are frequently seen in the writing of the poet or the musician. They indicate intuition—a lifting motion of the hand as the writing is done.

Tapering Handwriting in which the writing begins small and becomes larger shows straightforwardness and honesty. It typifies the conscientious individual who begins to express himself more and more plainly as he proceeds. In reverse, with the letters growing smaller, the writing betrays a selfish disposition, of the person who employs considerable reserve. 

Varying Size indicates the versatile individual, who is fanciful and of a changing disposition. Such a person has the faculty for doing many things, but is lacking in poise. 

Plain Handwriting, observed particularly in capital letters and in signatures, shows the modest, unassuming person; often one of literary ability who does not boast of his accomplishments. 117

CHARACTER READING 

Fancy Handwriting, which is likewise most evident in capital letters and in signatures, is the trade-mark of the egotist, who gives undue importance to his small deeds and actions. 

Closed Letters, such as a, b, d, o, r, and s show a discreet temperament, indicating the person who keeps a great many thoughts to himself.

Open Letters of the types just mentioned show the individual who wants to tell all he knows, and who frequently finds himself in a dilemma.

Unfinished Letters have a peculiar significance. These relate particularly to the elimination of dots over the letters i and j, or the failure to cross the letter t.

If this prevails throughout the writing, it is generally a sign that the individual has small regard for conventions, and is not particular about the impression he creates. 

When it occurs frequently, but not always, it is a sign of forgetfulness; of the person whose mind is preoccupied and who will neglect many things without meaning to do so. 

CHARACTER CHARTS 

The results of Character Reading may be divided into three sections. The first may be called the Preliminary Survey, covering 

Masculine and Feminine Traits, Color, etc. It deals with general distinctions.

The second section covers Form and Profile. It enables the student to make good classifications and to place his subjects under headings which are definite, with important specific traits. 

The third section is Physiognomy, which reveals very essential differences in individuals, and is the most intriguing of all studies; yet productive of many sub-divisions. 

The material following Physiognomy stands as a series of corollaries to the main phases of Character Analysis. 

It is with the second section that group results can be obtained. If an employer is hiring a man; if two men are having a business meeting; if a group of people must be studied quickly in order to pick out persons for certain tasks, Formation and Profile serve as the basic form of judgment. 

If a man is planning a project, he will consider the merits of various materials and sum them up according to their values. 

This can be done with individuals through Character Analysis. 118

The accompanying charts show the method-with brief summaries of the qualities that exist.

By running down the lists in these charts, it is not at all difficult to obtain a good basic judgment of the individual. From a skeleton outline one can build up an unerring picture, and with observations of Physiognomy-notations of Chirognomy, etc., the finest details may be filled in.

There are three ways in which information on the individual have an important bearing. (1) Advice and Guidance. (2)

Business or Social Relationship. (3) Employment. Each of these will be discussed separately. 

As a preliminary, we can consider the usefulness of Character

Reading in a general way, in reference to the Charts just shown. 

Suppose that you have heard of a book or publication that you believe will be of value to you. You obtain a prospectus.

Of course it describes the good qualities of the book. But most important are the specifications: binding, number of pages, size, price, etc.

So it is with the individual. You may have heard wonderful accounts of him; he may bear them up by his own statements. 

But, after all, you want specifications. You will find them by using the Charts. 

Suppose you are purchasing an automobile. You hear what the salesman has to say; you see the car; you learn its specifications. But if you are wise, you look for faults, or listen to the advice of an impartial person who has had experience with automobiles. In other words, a negative study, or critical analysis is useful to you.

Hence we can form Charts showing the weaknesses indicated by Formation and Profile.

With these rather undesirable qualities as a guide, the Character Analyst can find the faults that are apt to be present in the individual, and can make allowances for his negative characteristics.

VOCATIONAL GUIDANCE 

Choosing an occupation is an extremely difficult problem. 

The young man who seeks advice regarding a particular field in which to work is usually rewarded with a negative response. "Stay out of it," is the familiar advice given by the lawyer, the doctor, and the men who have succeeded in many business lines. 

What are the reasons for such advice? We do not have to look far to find them. The man who has been a failure will, of course, belittle his own occupation rather than himself. Some of those who have been successful feel that the effort they have expended has been too great in proportion to the gain. Some are candid enough to admit that they have been lucky, and that if they had met certain unexpected obstacles they would have failed. 

But others, who have been really successful, and who are satisfied with their achievements give negative advice purely because they have observed dozens who were failures for every one who has been successful.

Yet the young man or the young woman who must work must also of necessity adopt some vocation. Business and professions must move on and they need human talent. No matter how over-crowded they may be, there will always be room at the top. 

There will always be failures, especially where there are more persons engaged in an enterprise than that enterprise can support. 

But there are successful persons in every field. The unfortunate part of failure is that it is so often unnecessary. Persons of good 122

intelligence and desire do not always succeed; and the cold facts are that they have chosen the wrong vocation. 

The person who seeks a job wants employment, primarily. 

He or she is often ready and willing to take the first opportunity that presents itself. Such jobs are usually considered in the light of money-making propositions. The occupation is regarded purely as a method of gaining a living, and real interest lies outside of work. The person who works at such a job is unfortunate. 

Men as a rule want progressive work. If they can put interest into their labors they will become efficient and useful. If they do not like their jobs they will simply go through the motions. That is why so many workers are strong for unions. They admit that they want to confine themselves to a trade that they have learned and they want to make sure of their positions, fearing reduced wages or backward tendencies; seeking shorter hours with the same pay, so that they can put less time on the work that to them is a necessary evil. From the standpoint of the individual, it is right that he should gain the maximum of return for the minimum of effort, and the impartial person sympathizes with the employe-not with the employer. But the fact that a person is thinking of an occupation in a small way as though it were the only possible future in life is proof in itself that the person is not suited to the vocation.

To say that eighty-five per cent of the people in America are in the wrong occupation would be to make a very conservative estimate. The only ones who are properly occupied are those who are making active progress, and this does not mean the persons who are waiting around for someone in a higher position to die or resign. The man who is in the right vocation is one who can see success and achievement ahead. There are very few who have that privilege.

Women, in contrast to men, seldom suffer from pangs of desire for better occupation. When employed in unsuitable jobs they work regularly and do what is expected, but they are generally inefficient. Plenty of young women are inured to routine jobs with no thought of progress, and are chiefly concerned about the opportunities for marriage that will enable them to quit work. 

People loathe their jobs as a rule, and that is the cause of much of their misery. The reason that employers are slow to grant requests for higher wages is because they have their troubles also, and realize that the workers are nowhere near 100 per cent efficient. 123

CHARACTER READING 

The reason for such conditions is that the majority of young men and women drift into occupations. They take up the first jobs that they can obtain; if they shift, it is usually because they see immediate better return, not because they want to display more interest and effort.

Their choice of occupation is unscientific. If they have the ability to seek other work when they require it, they go at the problem with the idea to try a job and thus learn whether or not they are fitted for it.

Millions of young Americans are now being educated, and a great percentage of them have no idea whatever as to their future after they leave school or college. Each year a portion of these millions are thrust into the world with nothing more than a diploma to guide them. They want jobs: if they need work badly, they take anything, and begin at the bottom of businesses to which they may or may not be suited-usually the latter. Those who are somewhat independent financially find work that suits their fancy, without considering their capabilities. 

Others realize long beforehand that definite work is valuable; so they gain their training in technical schools or business colleges; or they study to be doctors, lawyers, dentists or seek some other profession. They do not realize that when they step out into the world they will have a long struggle ahead. They rely upon the importance of their proposed profession to carry them; not upon their own suitability and individuality. As a result, many of them are hopelessly lost before they begin. 

The same is true of those who have engaged in occupations to which they are not suited. They often realize that they have the wrong job; sometimes they condemn themselves for lack of ability; other times they blame their environment. They are afraid to change their vocations because they have known nothing but uncertainty.

Sales jobs are a tantalizing lure that bring difficulties to thousands of capable men and women. It is not difficult to obtain work selling on commission; there are always the examples of those who are "making good"; and there is the attraction of independence and freedom from routine. The result is that about one per cent of the active salesmen in America are really successful. 

The others merely get by, often shifting from one company to another. "Get in the selling end-that's where the money is made" is old advice about the only positive advice one hears. 

The reason large earnings are made by competent salesmen is 124

because the percentage of inefficiency in this type of work is so much greater than in any other. Those who sell have to get results to survive, for their work can be measured, in dollars and cents.

Every person of reasonable ability, moderate education and ordinary ambition has a proper place in the occupational world, where he or she will be happy and reasonably efficient. But lack of judgment, circumstances, and bewilderment prevent many persons from finding their appointed stations. Sentimental reasons lead some to take up clerical work and "white collar jobs" when they are suited to mechanical labors. Others take up the businesses or continue the professions in which their fathers were successful, and promptly become failures. Persons of real genius lose their opportunity for development through unsound choice, poor advice and lack of definite ambition; and humanity suffers a loss as well as the individual. 

A square plug won't fit into a round hole; but that doesn't mean that the plug is no good. Similarly the man or woman who is incapable of filling one job effectively is not a failure. His or her work may lie in a much more productive field. 

Look at the men and women who have succeeded. They can often tell a story of difficulties in their early career. The realization of their own ability and the final choice of their proper work has brought them to the threshold of achievement. A successful advertising man began as a journalist; a competent lawyer was originally a draughtsman-there are thousands of cases where self-realization has led to new fields of achievement. 

Obviously the earlier that one can find the proper field, the better. There are many who know that they are now misplaced, but are doubtful as to their abilities in other occupations. Those who still have opportunity to make a choice of work should do it. 

How? Through analysis of character. Natural traits and hidden talents are the assets of the individual. Men and women can analyze their own abilities, and they can give effective advice. through others by scientific Character Reading.

We have discussed problems that are facts, and we have done so without restraint, because the solution of such problems is within the reader's reach. The Vocational Chart that follows is a fundamental guide to proper choice of occupation. 125

CHARACTER READING VOCATIONAL CHART 

Explanatory: This Chart is based upon the previous Charts of Formation, Head, and Profile. It shows the types of Forms, Heads and Profiles that are particularly adapted to certain occupations, and also the types that are unsuited to the same vocations. 

To be really fitted for a certain work, the individual should have a greater number of qualifications than disqualifications. 

Lack of either shows doubtful or indifferent prospects. Presence of one or more qualifications with no disqualifications is an excellent sign; absence of qualifications and presence of one or more disqualifications shows that the individual is very probably unsuited to the vocation.

There are peculiar exceptions to the rule, which can usually be explained or discovered by a study of the individual's Physiognomy; but these are exceedingly rare. 

The Mental-Motive-Vital Type, and the Balanced Head are signs of ordinary ability in most occupations, and hence are seldom qualifications or disqualifications.
